All books in one




















Between Rage and Reason
Kiminou Knox








Chapter 1: The Voice from the Grave
Death has a way of clarifying things.
That's the first thing I learned after I died how sharp everything becomes when you're no longer part of it. Like watching a movie you've seen a dozen times and suddenly noticing details in the background you never caught before. The subtle expressions crossing someone's face. The way light falls through a window. The small choices that, strung together like beads on a wire, led inevitably to the end.
My funeral was on a Tuesday. November 2nd, 2035. Eight days after I died.
I watched it from somewhere above, detached yet present, like I was suspended from the ceiling of the funeral home. The place was one of those modern buildings with too much glass and not enough soul. My parents had chosen it because it was close to home, not because it suited me. Nothing about that day suited me.
My brother Tyler sat in the front row, his broad shoulders hunched forward, his eyes hollow caves in his face. He hadn't slept since it happened I could tell. His jaw twitched every few minutes, that same nervous tic I'd seen a thousand times growing up. Only now it wasn't from excitement or anticipation. It was from something darker.
My parents flanked him like broken bookends. Mom in a black dress I'd never seen before, her hands clutching a tissue that had long since been shredded to pieces. Dad staring straight ahead, his face a mask of composure that didn't quite hide the trembling of his lower lip. They looked older than they had just two weeks ago. Grief had aged them overnight.
Behind them sat a strange collection of people from different corners of my life. High school friends I hadn't spoken to in years. Coworkers from the warehouse where I'd been working since dropping out of community college. A few distant relatives who probably couldn't have picked me out of a lineup before today but were now dabbing at their eyes like we'd been close.
And in the back row, almost hidden behind a large floral arrangement, sat Mr. and Mrs. Mendoza. Javier's parents. They hadn't come to mourn me they'd come to make sure I was really dead. To see with their own eyes that one of the monsters who took their son was gone. I couldn't blame them for that.
The pastor spoke about me in vague, pleasant terms. He'd never met me, of course. Called me a "young man taken too soon" and spoke of "God's mysterious ways." If God's ways included letting a twenty-three-year-old die with his skull cracked open on the floor of a decrepit cabin in the woods, then mysterious was certainly one word for it.
No one mentioned how I died. The newspaper had reported it clinically: "Kevin Reese, 23, found deceased at the site where kidnapping victim Javier Mendoza, 17, had been held captive." They didn't say my own brother had killed me. They didn't say I'd been trying to save Javier. Some truths are too complicated for obituaries.
I died on October 25th, 2035. The day everything went wrong. Though really, everything had started going wrong weeks before that. I just hadn't been paying attention.
________________


The night of October 25th was cold. The kind of cold that gets under your skin and makes your bones ache. Fog hung so thick you could barely see twenty feet ahead, nature's own security blanket. I remember looking at each of their faces as we sat in the stolen van, parked half a block from the Mendoza estate. They were scared… I was too.
Tyler, my big brother, stood there like some discount movie villain, trying to look tough. But I knew that twitch in his jaw he was scared shitless too. Conner kept checking his phone, probably hoping for some miracle text that would call this whole thing off. Brad... poor Brad was practically dancing, either from nerves or because he needed to pee. Again.
"Everyone clear on the plan?" Tyler asked, his voice steady despite the fear I could see in his eyes. He was twenty-six, three years older than me, but in that moment he seemed both older and younger somehow a boy playing at being a man, making decisions too big for him to handle.
"We've been over it a dozen times," Conner said, not looking up from his phone. "We grab the kid, we bring him to the cabin, we make the call, we get paid. Simple."
Nothing about it was simple, but none of us said that out loud.
Brad shifted his weight from one foot to the other. "What if there's security? The place is huge."
"There's no security," Tyler said with the confidence of someone who'd done his homework. And he had that was the thing about Tyler. He was thorough. He'd spent weeks watching the Mendoza house, learning their routines, identifying vulnerabilities. "Just the housekeeper, and she leaves at eight. The parents are at some charity gala downtown until midnight. The kid will be alone."
The kid. Javier. Seventeen years old. Son of Eduardo Mendoza, CEO of Instant Pizza Corp, a man worth north of fifty million dollars. A man who, according to Tyler's research, would pay handsomely to get his only son back.
Five million dollars. That was the number Tyler had settled on. Enough to change our lives, not enough to make Mendoza hesitate. Split four ways, it would give us each $1.25 million. Enough for me to pay off my student loans and start over somewhere new. Enough for Tyler to clear his gambling debts and have plenty left over. Enough for Conner to pay for his mom's cancer treatments. Enough for Brad to... well, Brad never really said what he needed the money for. He just needed it, like we all did.
"It's almost time," Tyler said, checking his watch. "Javier should be getting back from debate team practice any minute now."
That's how detailed Tyler's intelligence was he knew the kid's after-school schedule. Debate team on Mondays and Thursdays. Home by 8:15 PM. Alone in that big house until his parents returned.
I pulled my ski mask down over my face, the fabric immediately making my skin itch. The others did the same. Four anonymous figures in a stolen van on a foggy night. The perfect crime, Tyler had said. No one would see us. No one would know who we were. We'd be ghosts.
I didn't feel like a ghost then. I felt all too solid, all too present, my heart hammering against my ribs like it was trying to break free. Like it knew something I didn't.
"There he is," Conner whispered, pointing through the fog.
A slender figure emerged from the mist, walking up the long driveway toward the Mendoza mansion. Javier had his headphones on, his backpack slung over one shoulder. He looked tired. Normal. Just a kid coming home from school, looking forward to a quiet night alone.
He had no idea his life was about to change forever. Neither did I.
"Let's go," Tyler said, and we moved.
The four of us slipped out of the van and crossed the street, keeping to the shadows. The fog helped, wrapping around us like an accomplice. We reached the edge of the Mendoza property just as Javier was punching the security code into the gate.
Tyler nodded at Brad, who moved forward with surprising quickness for a guy his size. He clamped one hand over Javier's mouth and wrapped his other arm around the boy's chest, lifting him off his feet. Javier's eyes went wide with terror, his muffled screams barely audible through Brad's gloved hand.
I was supposed to grab his legs, but I froze. For one terrible second, I just stood there, watching this kid this stranger having the worst moment of his life. His eyes locked with mine through the holes in my mask, and I saw something there that I wasn't prepared for. Not just fear, but a question. Why?
"Kevin, move!" Tyler hissed, and I snapped back to reality, grabbing Javier's kicking legs as we carried him back to the van.
Conner had the side door open, and we bundled Javier inside. Tyler climbed in after him, a syringe in his hand. The sedative was another part of his meticulous plan keep the kid unconscious during transport, minimize the risk.
"Hold him still," Tyler ordered, and Brad and I pinned Javier's struggling form to the floor of the van. I could feel him trembling beneath my hands, his body rigid with terror.
Tyler found a vein in Javier's arm and pushed the needle in. The boy's struggles gradually weakened, his eyes growing unfocused, then closing altogether. His body went limp beneath my hands.
"Is he okay?" I asked, my voice sounding strange and distant to my own ears.
"He's fine," Tyler said, checking Javier's pulse. "He'll be out for a couple of hours. Plenty of time to get to the cabin."
Conner slid into the driver's seat, and Brad took the passenger side. Tyler and I stayed in the back with Javier, who now looked unnervingly peaceful, like he was just taking a nap on the dirty floor of a stolen van.
As Conner pulled away from the curb, merging into the foggy night, I couldn't shake the feeling that we'd just crossed a line we could never uncross. That we'd set something in motion that would consume us all.
I was right about that, at least.
________________


The drive to the cabin took almost an hour, winding through the city and then up into the mountains that ringed it. No one spoke much. The radio played softly some pop song about love and loss that seemed obscenely inappropriate given what we were doing.
I kept my eyes on Javier, watching the rise and fall of his chest to make sure he was still breathing. The sedative Tyler had used was something he'd gotten from a veterinarian friend designed for dogs, but "safe for humans in the right dose," he'd assured us. Looking at Javier's pale face, I wasn't so sure.
"He's fine," Tyler said, noticing my concern. "Stop worrying."
But worrying was all I could do. My mind raced through all the things that could go wrong. What if the sedative was too strong? What if Javier had some medical condition we didn't know about? What if his parents came home early and already knew he was missing? What if the police were already looking for us?
"You're thinking too loud," Tyler said, nudging me with his elbow. "Relax. Everything's going according to plan."
That was Tyler always confident, always in control. Or at least, always pretending to be. I'd spent my whole life following his lead, trusting that he knew what he was doing. Even when the things he was doing were questionable at best, criminal at worst.
But this was different. This wasn't shoplifting from the mall or selling fake IDs to college kids. This was kidnapping. This was a felony that could put us away for decades. This was a line I never thought we'd cross.
Yet here I was, crossing it.
The cabin came into view as Conner navigated the van up the narrow dirt road that led to it. It belonged to Brad's uncle, or so Brad claimed. More likely it was just abandoned, forgotten by whoever had built it decades ago. It sat in a small clearing, surrounded by dense forest on three sides. The perfect hiding place miles from the nearest neighbor, accessible only by the single dirt road we'd just traveled.
"Home sweet home," Conner muttered as he parked the van behind the cabin, out of sight from the road.
The cabin was a single-story structure, weathered by years of mountain winters. The porch sagged in the middle, and several of the windows were boarded up. It looked like the setting of a horror movie, which I suppose it was, in a way.
"Brad, help me with him," Tyler said, gesturing to Javier's unconscious form.
Brad climbed out of the front seat and came around to the back. Together, he and Tyler lifted Javier and carried him toward the cabin. I followed behind, carrying the duffel bag that contained our supplies rope, duct tape, burner phones, and a few other items Tyler had deemed essential for our operation.
Conner unlocked the cabin door, which creaked open to reveal a musty, dark interior. He fumbled for the light switch, and a single bare bulb flickered to life, casting harsh shadows across the room.
The inside of the cabin was sparse a sagging couch, a rickety table with four mismatched chairs, a kitchenette with an ancient refrigerator that hummed loudly. A short hallway led to two small bedrooms and a bathroom with questionable plumbing.
Tyler and Brad carried Javier to one of the bedrooms and laid him on the twin bed that occupied most of the space. The room had a single window, which Tyler had boarded up from the inside days earlier, when we'd come to prepare the place.
"Get the rope," Tyler instructed, and I handed him the coil from the duffel bag.
I watched as Tyler efficiently bound Javier's wrists and ankles to the bed frame. Not too tight he was careful about that. "We don't want to hurt him," he'd explained during the planning. "We just need to keep him secure."
But looking at Javier tied to the bed, his face still slack from the sedative, "secure" seemed like a euphemism for something much darker.
"He'll wake up in an hour or so," Tyler said, checking his watch. "One of us should stay with him, make sure he doesn't panic."
"I'll do it," I volunteered, surprising myself as much as Tyler.
He raised an eyebrow but nodded. "Fine. The rest of us will get things set up for tomorrow. That's when the real work begins."
The real work. The ransom demand. The waiting. The exchange. The aftermath.
As Tyler, Brad, and Conner left the room, closing the door behind them, I sat in the single chair in the corner, watching Javier's chest rise and fall. In the harsh light of the bare bulb, he looked younger than seventeen. His dark hair fell across his forehead, and there was a small scar above his right eyebrow. He wore a private school uniform navy blazer, gray slacks, white shirt. The blazer was rumpled now, the shirt coming untucked.
He was just a kid. A kid with parents who loved him, friends who would miss him, a future that had been bright and promising until we came along and derailed it.
What were we doing? What was I doing?
I'd asked myself these questions before, of course. During the planning stages, during the surveillance, during the drive to the Mendoza estate. But sitting there in that dingy room, watching our victim sleep, the questions took on a new urgency, a new weight.
Tyler had an answer ready whenever I expressed doubts. "It's just business," he'd say. "No one gets hurt, everyone gets paid." He made it sound simple, logical, almost ethical in its own twisted way.
But there was nothing simple about the knot in my stomach as I watched Javier stir slightly, his eyelids fluttering as the sedative began to wear off. Nothing logical about the shame that burned in my chest. Nothing ethical about any of this.
I should have said no from the beginning. I should have walked away when Tyler first proposed the plan. I should have done anything but sit in that chair, waiting for our victim to wake up and face the nightmare we'd created for him.
But I didn't. I stayed. I participated. I crossed the line.
And that's the thing about lines once you cross them, you can never really go back. You can only go forward, deeper into whatever darkness lies on the other side.
Javier moaned softly, his head turning to one side. He was waking up. In a few minutes, he would open his eyes to find himself bound to a bed in a strange place, surrounded by strangers in masks who wanted to use him as a pawn in their desperate scheme.
In a few minutes, the real nightmare would begin.
For all of us.
________________


I know how this story ends. I've known from the beginning, from the first words I spoke to you from beyond the veil of death. There's no suspense for me, no wondering how things will turn out.
But even knowing the ending, I'm compelled to tell the story. To trace the path that led from that foggy night to my final moments on the cabin floor. To understand, if understanding is possible, how everything went so wrong.
Maybe that's what the afterlife is not clouds and harps or fire and brimstone, but the chance to finally see the pattern of your life, to follow the thread of your choices back to their source. To witness without the ability to change.
Or maybe I'm just not ready to let go. Maybe I'm lingering because there are things left unresolved, words left unsaid. Maybe I'm here because someone needs to bear witness to what happened in those woods, to make sure the truth isn't buried along with my body.
Whatever the reason, I'm here. Watching. Remembering. Telling you this story from its beginning, even though I already know its end.
I died on October 25th, 2035, in a cabin in the woods, at the hands of my own brother. But the path to that moment began long before, with small choices that seemed insignificant at the time. A missed opportunity. A debt unpaid. A loyalty misplaced. A moral compromise.
Some plans are doomed before they begin. Ours was dead on arrival, we just didn't know it yet.
But we were about to find out.
Chapter 2: The Plan in Motion
Three weeks before it all went to hell, I was sitting on Tyler's threadbare couch, nursing a lukewarm beer and watching my brother pace the cramped living room of his apartment like a caged animal. The place smelled like stale cigarettes and desperation a studio in the worst part of town, where the rent was cheap and the questions were few. A far cry from the life Tyler had always said he deserved.
"Five million," Tyler said, his eyes gleaming with an intensity that made me uneasy. "That's what we ask for. Not so much that they'll hesitate, not so little that it's not worth the risk."
I shifted uncomfortably, the springs of the ancient couch digging into my thighs. "Tyler, this is insane. We're talking about kidnapping. Actual kidnapping. Like, the kind of shit people go to prison for decades over."
He stopped pacing and fixed me with that look the one he'd been giving me since we were kids, the one that made me feel simultaneously stupid and disloyal for questioning him.
"You got a better idea, little brother? Because last I checked, you were about to get kicked out of community college for not paying tuition. Again." He gestured around his dismal apartment. "You want to end up like this? Working two shit jobs and still barely making rent?"
He wasn't wrong. I was three months behind on tuition payments, working nights at a warehouse and days at a gas station, and still drowning. The community college was the last chance I had at something resembling a future, and it was slipping through my fingers like sand.
"There's gotta be another way," I said, but even to my own ears, it sounded weak.
Tyler snorted. "Yeah? Like what? A bank loan with your credit score? Another minimum wage job? Face it, Kev, the system is rigged against guys like us. Always has been."
He sat down next to me, the couch groaning under our combined weight. His voice softened, taking on that persuasive tone he used when he really wanted something.
"Look, I've thought this through. It's clean, it's simple. We take the kid, we get the money, we let him go. Nobody gets hurt. These people have insurance for this kind of thing. It's basically a victimless crime."
I almost laughed at that. "A victimless crime? We're talking about grabbing some teenager and holding him against his will. How is that victimless?"
Tyler waved his hand dismissively. "He'll be scared for a few days, then he goes home to his mansion and his perfect life. Meanwhile, you get to stay in school, I get these guys off my back, and we all get a fresh start." He leaned closer, his eyes boring into mine. "One bad thing, Kev. We do one bad thing, and all our problems go away."
One bad thing. As if a single action could be isolated from everything that came before and after it. As if we could step into darkness and then simply step back out, unchanged.
I should have walked out then. Should have told him he was crazy and never looked back. But I didn't. Because beneath my moral objections was a darker truth I didn't want to face: part of me was tempted. Part of me wanted the easy way out that Tyler was offering.
"Who else is in on this?" I asked, and with that question, I took my first step across the line.
Tyler's face broke into a grin. "Conner and Brad. They're meeting us here in an hour."
________________


Conner arrived first, looking like he always did tall, thin, with the perpetually haunted expression of someone who's seen too much too young. He'd been Tyler's friend since high school, the smart one who'd had a scholarship to State before his mom got sick and he had to drop out to care for her.
"Kevin," he nodded at me, his voice neutral. We weren't friends, exactly, but we weren't enemies either. We existed in the same orbit because of Tyler, nothing more.
Brad showed up twenty minutes late, as usual. Where Conner was all angles and nervous energy, Brad was solid, with the build of someone who'd played football in high school and had been coasting on that peak ever since. He had a six-pack of expensive beer the kind none of us could really afford and a smile that didn't quite reach his eyes.
"The gang's all here," he said, handing out the beers. "Let's get rich."
Tyler had prepared a presentation of sorts printouts and maps spread across his rickety coffee table, all centered around one person: Javier Mendoza.
"Eduardo Mendoza, CEO of Instant Pizza Corp. Net worth estimated at fifty-three million," Tyler began, pointing to a newspaper clipping of a middle-aged Hispanic man in an expensive suit. "Self-made man, started with one pizza shop and built an empire. Now they've got those automated pizza machines in every mall in America."
He shifted to another photo, this one showing a woman with carefully styled hair and a practiced smile. "His wife, Maria. Former beauty queen, now runs a charity for underprivileged kids. Ironic, right?"
None of us laughed at his attempt at humor.
Tyler cleared his throat and moved on to the next photo. "And this is their son, Javier. Seventeen years old. Junior at St. Augustine Prep. Only child."
The photo showed a teenager with his father's strong jaw and his mother's warm eyes. He was wearing a school uniform and had the awkward smile of someone who hadn't grown into his features yet. He looked... normal. Just a kid.
"Why him?" I asked, though I already knew the answer.
"Because he's worth five million to his parents," Tyler said bluntly. "And because he's an easy target. Private school kid, predictable schedule, minimal security."
He pulled out a hand-drawn schedule. "He's got debate team on Mondays and Thursdays. Gets home around 8:15. Parents are usually out late those nights business dinners, charity events, whatever rich people do. The housekeeper leaves at 8. Which means he's alone in that big house for hours."
The methodical way Tyler laid it all out made my stomach turn. He'd been watching this kid, studying him like a predator studies prey.
"The house has an alarm system, but no private security," Tyler continued. "No dogs. No bodyguards. Just a teenager with rich parents who think nothing bad could ever happen in their gated community."
"How do we know they'll pay?" Brad asked, already counting the money in his head.
Tyler smiled. "Because Eduardo Mendoza loves his son more than anything. There was a profile in Business Monthly last year called Javier his 'greatest achievement.' Trust me, he'll pay."
"And if he calls the cops instead?" Conner asked, always the pragmatist.
"We make it clear that's not an option," Tyler said, his voice hardening slightly. "But he won't. These types never do. They don't trust the police to handle it right. They'll pay quietly and be grateful when they get their kid back."
I looked around at the three of them Tyler with his desperate ambition, Conner with his quiet calculation, Brad with his greedy anticipation and felt suddenly, overwhelmingly alone.
"Five million split four ways," Brad said, whistling low. "That's one-point-two-five mil each. Jesus."
"What do you need the money for anyway?" I asked him, genuinely curious. Tyler had his gambling debts, Conner had his mom's medical bills, I had my tuition. But Brad had never mentioned any financial problems.
He shrugged, taking a long pull from his beer. "I'm tired of being nobody. Want to buy a business, be my own boss for once. Maybe a gym or something."
It seemed like such a mundane dream to risk everything for. But then, weren't all our dreams mundane in their own way? We weren't asking for mansions or private jets. Just a chance to climb one rung up the ladder, to escape the gravitational pull of poverty that kept us tethered to lives we hadn't chosen.
"So we're really doing this?" Conner asked, his eyes on Tyler.
Tyler nodded slowly. "We're really doing this. But only if everyone's in. One hundred percent. No hesitation, no backing out later."
Three pairs of eyes turned to me, and I felt the weight of their expectation like a physical pressure.
"I don't know," I said, staring at the photo of Javier. "It seems like there should be another way."
Tyler's face darkened. "There isn't. Not for guys like us. The world doesn't give people like us second chances or lucky breaks. We have to take what we need."
"And what about him?" I nodded toward Javier's picture. "What about his second chances?"
"He was born with all the chances in the world," Tyler said dismissively. "He'll be fine. A little scared, but fine."
I wasn't convinced, but I also wasn't strong enough to walk away. Not from Tyler, not from the promise of solving all my problems with "one bad thing."
"Okay," I said finally. "I'm in."
The words tasted like ash in my mouth.
________________


The next three weeks were a blur of preparation. Tyler approached the kidnapping like a military operation, assigning tasks, establishing protocols, anticipating contingencies. If he'd applied that kind of focus and intelligence to legitimate pursuits, he might have been something. We all might have been.
Conner was in charge of the technical aspects burner phones, the voice disguiser for ransom calls, the encrypted messaging app we'd use to communicate. Brad handled logistics securing the cabin, getting the van, mapping escape routes. Tyler oversaw everything, his mind constantly working, refining, adjusting.
And me? I was the reluctant assistant, helping with surveillance, gathering supplies, asking questions no one wanted to answer.
"What if he needs medication?" I asked one night as we sat in the stolen van, watching Javier's house from a distance.
Tyler sighed. "We'll get it for him if he does. We're not monsters, Kev."
But weren't we? What would you call four men plotting to snatch a teenager from his life and hold him for ransom?
I tried to rationalize it to myself. Javier would be scared but unharmed. His parents would pay. He'd go home. We'd disappear with the money. A temporary trauma in exchange for our freedom. A fair trade, according to Tyler's moral calculus.
But every time we watched Javier walking to school, laughing with friends, living his normal teenage life the knot in my stomach grew tighter. This wasn't some abstract concept anymore. This was a real person whose life we were about to shatter.
One afternoon, I followed Javier to a coffee shop after school. He sat alone at a table, reading a book and occasionally making notes in the margin. I ordered a coffee and sat at a nearby table, pretending to look at my phone while actually studying our target.
He seemed serious, focused. The book was something about medical ethics heavy reading for a high school kid. At one point, he got a call and his face lit up with a smile. "Hey, Dad," he said, and launched into an excited description of some debate team victory. The love and pride in his voice when he talked about his father made me feel physically ill.
This was the relationship we were planning to exploit. This bond between father and son that Tyler was so certain would translate into a multimillion-dollar payout.
I left the coffee shop without finishing my drink, unable to bear the weight of what we were planning to do.
________________


A week before the kidnapping, I tried to back out.
We were at the cabin, preparing it for Javier's "stay." Brad was boarding up windows while Conner set up the burner phones. Tyler and I were in what would be Javier's room, assembling the bed frame we'd bought secondhand.
"I can't do this," I said quietly, setting down the screwdriver I'd been using. "It's wrong, Tyler. You know it's wrong."
Tyler continued working, not looking at me. "We've been over this. It's too late to back out now."
"It's not too late," I insisted. "We haven't done anything yet. We can just walk away."
He finally looked up, his expression a mixture of disappointment and anger. "Walk away to what, Kevin? You think your problems are just going to solve themselves? You think mine are?"
"We'll figure something out. Something that doesn't involve ruining some kid's life."
Tyler stood up abruptly, towering over me. "You know what those guys I owe money to said they'd do if I don't pay? They said they'd break my legs. And then they'd come for you and Mom and Dad."
I blinked, taken aback. "What? You never said "
"Because I was handling it!" he snapped. "That's what I do, Kevin. I handle things. I solve problems. I protect this family."
He ran a hand through his hair, his frustration evident. "Who got you your first job when Dad lost his? Who kept the lights on when Mom got sick? Who bailed you out when you got caught shoplifting? Me. It's always been me."
The guilt hit me like a physical blow. Tyler had always been there for me, had always played the role of protector. Even when we were kids, he'd taken beatings meant for me, had gone hungry so I could eat, had shouldered burdens I was too weak to carry.
"I know," I said softly. "But this is different."
"No, it's not," he insisted. "It's me doing what needs to be done to protect what matters. And what matters is family. You and me, we stick together. Always have, always will."
He put his hand on my shoulder, his voice softening. "I need you with me on this, little brother. I can't do it without you."
And there it was the emotional manipulation that had defined our relationship for as long as I could remember. The reminder of all I owed him, the appeal to family loyalty, the subtle implication that questioning him was a betrayal.
I should have been stronger. Should have seen through it. Should have walked away.
But I didn't.
"Okay," I said, picking up the screwdriver again. "I'm with you."
Tyler smiled, relief evident in his eyes. "Good. Because we're family, and family sticks together."
Family sticks together. It was our mantra, our excuse, our prison.
________________


The night before the kidnapping, we gathered in Tyler's apartment for one final review of the plan. The tension in the room was palpable, each of us dealing with it in our own way. Conner was methodically checking and rechecking the burner phones. Brad was drinking more than usual, his laugh too loud, his movements too expansive. Tyler was focused, almost serene, like a man who had accepted whatever was coming.
And me? I was a mess of contradictions terrified yet resigned, morally repulsed yet pragmatically committed. I'd made my choice, or rather, I'd let Tyler make it for me. Now all that remained was to see it through.
"Tomorrow night, 8:15," Tyler said, pointing to the circled date on his calendar. "Javier gets home from debate team. We grab him at the gate before he can enter the security code. Quick, clean, no witnesses."
"What if something goes wrong?" I asked, voicing the fear that had been growing in me for weeks.
Tyler gave me a reassuring smile that didn't reach his eyes. "Nothing will go wrong. We've planned for every contingency."
But you can't plan for everything. You can't anticipate every variable, every human reaction, every twist of fate. No matter how meticulous the plan, chaos always finds a way in.
"Once we have him, we drive straight to the cabin," Tyler continued. "No stops, no detours. Brad and Conner take the first watch while Kevin and I get rid of the van."
The stolen van was perhaps the riskiest part of the plan. We'd taken it from a shopping mall parking lot three days earlier, replaced the plates with stolen ones, and hidden it in a storage unit Tyler had rented under a fake name. After the kidnapping, we'd abandon it in a lake about twenty miles from the cabin, hopefully buying us time before the police could connect it to Javier's disappearance.
"The next morning, we make the ransom call," Tyler said. "Conner, that's on you and Brad. Use the voice disguiser, keep it short, be firm but not threatening. Five million, cryptocurrency, no police."
Conner nodded, his face grim. "And if they've already called the cops by then?"
"They won't have," Tyler said with that infuriating confidence of his. "Mendoza will want to handle this privately. Rich people always do."
"And then we wait," Brad said, taking another drink. "How long do you think before we get the money?"
Tyler shrugged. "A day, maybe two. Mendoza has the resources to move quickly. Once we confirm the payment, we let the kid go and we disappear. Separate ways, just like we discussed."
It all sounded so simple when he laid it out like that. A business transaction with slightly higher stakes than usual. No one gets hurt, everyone gets paid, life goes on.
But as I looked at Tyler, really looked at him, I saw something in his eyes I'd never seen before. A darkness, a coldness that made me wonder if I ever really knew my brother at all. It was gone in an instant, replaced by his usual confident expression, but it left me shaken.
"Get some sleep, everyone," Tyler said, standing up to signal the end of the meeting. "Tomorrow's a big day."
As Conner and Brad left, I lingered, still troubled by what I'd seen or thought I'd seen in Tyler's eyes.
"You okay?" he asked, noticing my hesitation.
"Yeah," I lied. "Just nervous, I guess."
He clapped me on the shoulder. "That's normal. But trust me, this time tomorrow, we'll be on our way to solving all our problems."
I nodded and turned to leave, but at the door, I paused. "Tyler, we're really going to let him go, right? After we get the money?"
For a moment, something flickered across his face annoyance, impatience, or something darker. But then he smiled, and he was my big brother again, the one who had always protected me.
"Of course we are," he said. "That's the plan."
I wanted to believe him. I needed to believe him. So I did, or pretended to.
"Okay," I said. "See you tomorrow."
As I walked back to my apartment through the cold night air, I couldn't shake the feeling that I was walking toward something I wouldn't be able to walk away from. That with each step, I was moving deeper into a darkness that would eventually consume me.
I was right about that. I just didn't know how right.
The day was October 25th, 2035, it was cold. The kind of cold that gets under your skin and makes your bones ache. Fog hung so thick you could barely see twenty feet ahead, nature's own security blanket.
I remember looking at each of their faces, they were scared… I was too. Tyler, my big brother, stood there like some discount movie villain, trying to look tough. But I knew that twitch in his jaw he was scared shitless too. Conner kept checking his phone, probably hoping for some miracle text that would call this whole thing off. Brad... poor Brad was practically dancing, either from nerves or because he needed to pee…Again.
And me? I was already a ghost, haunting the edges of a life I was about to throw away. I just didn't know it yet.
Chapter 3: The Kidnapping
The stolen van smelled like fear and fast food. Four hours into our stakeout, the interior had become a pungent cocktail of nervous sweat, cold french fries, and the cheap air freshener that had been hanging from the rearview mirror when we took it. The pine-scented cardboard tree swung gently with each of Tyler's impatient taps on the steering wheel, a hypnotic pendulum counting down the seconds to something terrible.
We'd been parked half a block from the Mendoza estate since 4:30 PM, watching the ornate iron gates through a gap in the fog that rolled in as the sun set. The mansion itself was barely visible just a suggestion of warm lights and wealth beyond the mist. It looked like something from another world, a place where people like us didn't exist except to deliver packages or fix plumbing.
"He's late," Tyler muttered, checking his watch for the dozenth time in the past hour. Debate team practice should have ended at 7:30. It was now approaching 8:20.
"Maybe they canceled," I said, trying not to sound hopeful. "Or maybe he got a ride with someone else."
Tyler shook his head. "He always walks. Rain or shine. Kid's got a routine."
The certainty in his voice made my skin crawl. How many days had Tyler spent watching Javier, learning his habits, plotting this moment? The level of obsession required was something I hadn't fully appreciated until now, sitting in this van, waiting to shatter a stranger's life.
Brad shifted in the passenger seat, the vinyl creaking beneath his weight. "I gotta pee again."
"You should've gone before we left," Conner said from beside me in the back, not looking up from his phone. The blue light illuminated his face from below, making him look ghoulish.
"I did go before we left," Brad hissed. "But that was four hours ago. And I drank that Big Gulp."
"Just hold it," Tyler snapped. "He'll be here any minute."
Brad muttered something under his breath but settled back into his seat, his discomfort adding another layer of tension to the already strained atmosphere.
I pulled my ski mask from my pocket and ran my thumb over the rough fabric. It was black, generic, the kind you could buy at any sporting goods store. Nothing special except for what we planned to use it for.
"Put those away," Tyler said, catching the movement in the rearview mirror. "Not until I say."
I stuffed the mask back into my pocket, my hands shaking slightly. The reality of what we were about to do was finally hitting me, a wave of nausea rising in my throat.
"I don't feel good about this," I said quietly.
Tyler's eyes met mine in the mirror, hard and unyielding. "We've been over this, Kevin. It's too late for second thoughts."
"It's not too late," I insisted. "We could just drive away right now. No one would ever know."
"And what about my debts?" Tyler demanded. "What about your tuition? Conner's mom's medical bills? We just forget about all that?"
I had no answer. The weight of our collective desperation hung in the van like another passenger, silent but insistent.
Conner looked up from his phone. "Someone's coming."
We all tensed, peering through the fog toward the gates of the Mendoza estate. A figure emerged from the mist, walking with the slightly hunched posture of someone carrying a heavy backpack. Even from this distance, I could see the headphones covering his ears, the slight bounce in his step suggesting he was walking in time to whatever music was playing.
Javier Mendoza. Seventeen years old. Only child of Eduardo and Maria Mendoza. Our target.
"That's him," Tyler said unnecessarily. "Masks on. Remember the plan."
We pulled the ski masks over our faces, the fabric immediately making my skin itch and my breathing feel restricted. Through the eyeholes, the world narrowed to a limited field of vision, like watching everything through a telescope.
"Now," Tyler said, and we moved.
The four of us slipped out of the van, closing the doors quietly behind us. The fog swallowed us immediately, providing cover as we crossed the street toward the Mendoza property. My heart hammered against my ribs so hard I was sure the others could hear it.
Javier had reached the gate and was punching in the security code, his back to us, completely unaware of our approach. Up close, he looked smaller than in the surveillance photos, his school uniform hanging slightly loose on his frame. He wasn't the spoiled, pampered rich kid I'd imagined. He was just a teenager, tired after a long day, wanting to go home.
Tyler nodded at Brad, who moved forward with surprising quickness for his size. But in his haste or perhaps due to his full bladder Brad's foot caught on a coiled garden hose near the gate. He stumbled, cursing loudly enough to make Javier turn.
For one frozen moment, we stared at each other through the fog four masked figures and one terrified teenager. Javier's eyes widened, his mouth opening to scream.
"Grab him!" Tyler hissed, and we lunged forward.
Brad recovered his balance and clamped a hand over Javier's mouth, muffling his shout. Conner was supposed to apply the chloroform, but in the chaos, he fumbled with the bottle, nearly dropping it.
"I can't get the cap off!" he whispered frantically.
Tyler swore and took matters into his own hands. With brutal efficiency, he punched Javier in the stomach, causing the boy to double over in Brad's grip, the air rushing from his lungs.
"Get him to the van," Tyler ordered, his voice cold and controlled despite the chaos.
Brad and I lifted Javier between us, his body surprisingly light as we half-carried, half-dragged him across the street. He struggled weakly, still trying to recover his breath, his eyes wild with terror above Brad's gloved hand.
Those eyes. God, those eyes. They locked onto mine through the holes in my mask, wide and pleading. I'd never seen fear like that before raw, primal, the kind that strips away everything but the most basic human instinct: survival.
In that moment, the abstract concept of "kidnapping" became horrifyingly concrete. This wasn't just a plan on paper or a theoretical discussion. This was violence. This was trauma being inflicted in real-time. This was wrong in a way I couldn't rationalize or explain away.
But it was too late to stop. We were already shoving Javier into the back of the van, already closing the doors, already sealing our fates along with his.
Conner finally got the chloroform bottle open and pressed a soaked rag to Javier's face. The boy thrashed, his muffled cries becoming more desperate. I held his legs down, feeling the tremors that ran through his body, hating myself with every second that passed.
"Por favor," Javier gasped when Brad briefly lifted his hand to allow Conner better access with the chloroform. "Please don't hurt me. My parents will give you whatever you want."
"Shut him up," Tyler snapped from the driver's seat as he started the engine.
Brad's hand clamped back over Javier's mouth, but not before he managed to cry out, "¡Mamá! ¡Papá! ¡Ayúdame!"
The desperation in his voice, the way he called for his parents in both English and Spanish, hit me like a physical blow. This wasn't the calculated risk of an adult or the abstract concept of a "target." This was a child, terrified and alone, calling for the people who had always protected him.
People who couldn't hear him now. People who had no idea their world was about to collapse.
The chloroform finally took effect, and Javier's struggles weakened, then ceased altogether. His body went limp beneath my hands, his eyes rolling back before closing. The sudden absence of resistance was jarring, like a storm that stops all at once, leaving an unnatural quiet in its wake.
"Is he breathing?" I asked, panic rising in my throat. "Tyler, is he breathing?"
Tyler reached back and pressed two fingers to Javier's neck. "He's fine. Pulse is steady. He'll be out for a couple of hours."
I sat back against the van wall, my legs shaking too badly to support me. What had we done? What had I done?
Tyler pulled away from the curb, driving carefully to avoid attracting attention. No one spoke. The only sounds were the hum of the engine, the occasional crackle of the police scanner Conner had set up, and Javier's soft, unconscious breathing.
I couldn't stop staring at him, this stranger whose life we had just violently interrupted. In sleep, the fear was gone from his face, replaced by a vulnerability that made my chest ache. He looked younger than seventeen, his dark lashes casting shadows on his cheeks, a small scar visible above his right eyebrow.
"Stop looking at him like that," Tyler said, catching my gaze in the rearview mirror. "He's not a person right now. He's a package. That's it."
But he was a person. That was the problem. He was a person with hopes and dreams and people who loved him. A person we had decided was worth less than our own desperate needs.
I pulled off my ski mask, suddenly unable to breathe through the restrictive fabric. The others followed suit, our faces emerging sweaty and pale in the dim light of the van.
"That didn't go exactly as planned," Conner said, his voice tight with tension.
Tyler shrugged, his eyes on the road. "We adapted. That's what matters. We got him, no one saw us, and now we're on our way to the cabin. Phase one complete."
He made it sound so clinical, so detached. As if what we'd just done was nothing more serious than a business transaction that had required some last-minute adjustments.
But there was nothing clinical about the unconscious teenager on the floor of our stolen van. Nothing detached about the violence we had just committed.
I looked out the window as Tyler navigated through the foggy streets, putting distance between us and the Mendoza estate. The city lights blurred through the mist, creating halos of color that seemed to float disconnected from their sources. It felt symbolic somehow everything familiar becoming strange and distorted, reality bending around the gravity of what we'd done.
No one spoke for a long time. Brad fidgeted uncomfortably, still needing to use the bathroom but not daring to ask Tyler to stop. Conner monitored the police scanner, his face tense with concentration. Tyler drove with mechanical precision, his eyes constantly checking the mirrors for any sign of pursuit.
And I sat with our victim, watching his chest rise and fall, wondering if this was what damnation felt like not fire and brimstone, but the cold, hollow knowledge that you've crossed a line you can never uncross.
About thirty minutes into the drive, Javier stirred slightly, moaning softly. His eyelids fluttered but didn't open.
"He's waking up," I said, alarm sharpening my voice.
Conner leaned forward with the chloroform rag, but Tyler shook his head.
"Save it. We're almost to the mountains. If he wakes up, just keep him quiet. We can't risk using too much of that stuff."
As if on cue, Javier's eyes opened, unfocused and confused. For a moment, he seemed disoriented, unable to process where he was or what had happened. Then memory returned, and with it, terror.
He tried to sit up, but his coordination was still affected by the chloroform. His movements were sluggish, uncoordinated. When he opened his mouth to scream, Brad clamped a hand over it again, muffling the sound.
"Easy, kid," Tyler said from the front seat, his voice unnervingly calm. "Don't make this harder than it needs to be."
Javier's eyes darted around the van, taking in our faces, no longer hidden by masks. I realized with a jolt that this was a mistake he could identify us now. But it was too late to do anything about it.
His gaze finally settled on me, sitting closest to him. There was something in that look beyond fear a question, an appeal to whatever humanity might still exist in me. It made me want to disappear, to become as insubstantial as the fog outside.
"Listen to me," Tyler said, his eyes meeting Javier's in the rearview mirror. "We're not going to hurt you. This is just business. Your father pays us, you go home. Simple as that. But if you cause problems, things get complicated. Understand?"
Javier nodded slightly against Brad's hand, his eyes still wide with fear but now calculating as well, assessing his situation, looking for opportunities.
"I'm going to tell Brad to take his hand away," Tyler continued. "If you scream or try anything stupid, there will be consequences. Nod if you understand."
Another slight nod.
"Good. Brad, let him go. Slowly."
Brad removed his hand cautiously, ready to clamp it back down at the first sign of trouble. But Javier remained silent, his breathing rapid but controlled.
"What do you want?" he asked finally, his voice hoarse and small.
"Money," Tyler said simply. "Five million dollars from your father. Once we have it, you go home. No harm done."
Javier swallowed hard. "My father will pay. Just... please don't hurt me."
The pleading in his voice made my stomach turn. This kid, who probably had never known real fear in his life, was now experiencing terror in its purest form. And we were the cause.
"No one's going to hurt you if you cooperate," Tyler assured him, his tone almost gentle. "This will all be over in a couple of days."
Javier's eyes found mine again, searching for confirmation or perhaps compassion. I couldn't hold his gaze. I looked away, out the window at the passing landscape.
We had left the city behind and were now climbing into the mountains, the road becoming steeper and more winding. The streetlights had given way to darkness broken only by our headlights and the occasional reflective marker at the edge of the road. The transition was jarring from the ordered grid of the city to the wild, unpredictable terrain of the mountains. From light to darkness. From civilization to wilderness.
It felt like a metaphor for what we were doing leaving behind the rules and structures of society, venturing into moral territory as wild and dangerous as the mountain roads we now traveled.
Javier had fallen silent, but I could hear his quiet, muffled sobs. He was trying to be brave, to maintain some dignity in an impossible situation. It made his fear all the more heartbreaking.
I wanted to say something to him to offer some comfort or reassurance. But what could I possibly say? "Sorry we kidnapped you, but we really need the money"? There were no words that could make this right, no explanation that could justify what we were doing.
So I said nothing, and the silence in the van grew heavier with each passing mile.
The road narrowed as we climbed higher, the trees pressing close on either side, their branches occasionally scraping against the sides of the van like skeletal fingers. The fog had thinned at this elevation, replaced by a clear, cold night sky scattered with stars that seemed to watch us with cold indifference.
"Almost there," Tyler said, breaking the silence that had stretched for miles.
He turned onto a barely visible dirt road, the van bouncing and jolting over the uneven surface. Javier was thrown against me by a particularly violent bump, and I instinctively steadied him before realizing what I was doing. Our eyes met briefly, and I saw something shift in his a recognition that perhaps I wasn't as committed to this as the others.
It was dangerous, that perception. Dangerous for him to hope, dangerous for me to show weakness. I pulled my hand away and moved slightly farther from him, hardening my expression.
The cabin appeared suddenly in our headlights a dark, hulking shape against the night sky. It looked even more decrepit than I remembered, the porch sagging in the middle, the windows like empty eye sockets staring out at the surrounding forest.
Tyler parked the van behind the cabin, out of sight from the road. The engine died, leaving us in a silence broken only by the ticking of the cooling metal and Javier's quiet, shuddering breaths.
"Home sweet home," Conner muttered, echoing my thoughts from our previous visit.
"Brad, Conner, get him inside," Tyler ordered. "Kevin and I will bring in the supplies."
Brad and Conner hauled Javier out of the van, his legs buckling slightly as they forced him to stand. The chloroform hadn't fully worn off, and his movements were still uncoordinated. They half-carried, half-dragged him toward the cabin, his feet stumbling over the uneven ground.
Tyler and I remained by the van, gathering the duffel bags containing food, water, and the other supplies we'd need for the next few days. When the others were out of earshot, Tyler turned to me.
"You need to get it together," he said, his voice low and intense. "I saw how you were looking at him. Like you felt sorry for him."
"I do feel sorry for him," I admitted. "Don't you?"
Tyler's expression hardened. "What I feel doesn't matter. What matters is getting the money and getting out clean. And that won't happen if you start going soft."
"I'm not going soft," I protested. "I'm just... I didn't expect it to feel like this."
"Like what?"
I struggled to find the words. "Like... like we're the bad guys in someone else's story."
Tyler snorted. "We are the bad guys in someone else's story. That's the point. But we're the heroes in our own. Remember why we're doing this."
He grabbed my shoulder, forcing me to look at him. "Five million dollars, Kevin. Freedom. A fresh start. All of it just a few days away. Don't lose sight of that now."
I nodded, not trusting myself to speak. Tyler was right about one thing we were the bad guys in Javier's story. What he didn't seem to understand was that being the villain in someone else's life meant being the villain, period. There was no perspective from which kidnapping an innocent teenager was heroic.
But I didn't say that. I just picked up the duffel bags and followed Tyler to the cabin, each step taking me further from the person I'd thought I was.
The interior of the cabin was as dismal as I remembered musty, cold, with shadows lurking in every corner. Brad had managed to get the generator running, and a single bare bulb cast harsh light over the main room. The ancient furniture looked even more decrepit in the unforgiving illumination, the sagging couch and mismatched chairs like the discarded remains of lives long abandoned.
Javier sat on one of the chairs, his wrists bound in front of him with zip ties. His school uniform was rumpled, his tie askew, his face pale with fear and the lingering effects of the chloroform. He looked up as Tyler and I entered, his eyes tracking our movements with wary attention.
"Get him some water," Tyler instructed, dropping his duffel bag on the rickety table. "And then secure him in the back room."
I took a bottle of water from my bag and approached Javier cautiously, as if he were a wounded animal that might either flee or attack. He flinched slightly as I got closer, then seemed to steel himself, straightening his shoulders in a small act of defiance.
"Here," I said, unscrewing the cap and holding the bottle to his lips. "Drink."
He hesitated, perhaps wondering if the water was drugged, then thirst won out. He drank deeply, water dribbling down his chin in his eagerness. When he'd had enough, I pulled the bottle away, recapping it.
"Thank you," he said quietly, the simple courtesy so incongruous with the situation that it caught me off guard.
Before I could respond, Tyler was beside us. "Time for bed, kid. You've had a long day."
Javier's eyes widened with renewed fear. "Please, just let me call my parents. Let them know I'm okay."
"Tomorrow," Tyler said, not unkindly. "We'll make contact tomorrow. For now, you need to rest."
He nodded at Brad, who hauled Javier to his feet and led him down the short hallway to the back bedroom. I followed, carrying a blanket and pillow from one of the duffel bags.
The bedroom was small and sparse just a twin bed bolted to the floor, a rickety chair, and a small table. The single window had been boarded up from the inside, eliminating any possibility of escape. It looked like a prison cell, which I suppose it was.
Brad pushed Javier onto the bed and began securing his ankles with another zip tie, this one looped through the metal frame of the bed. Javier didn't resist, his eyes fixed on some middle distance, perhaps retreating into himself as a form of psychological protection.
"Not too tight," I said to Brad. "We don't want to cut off his circulation."
Brad gave me a strange look but adjusted the zip tie slightly. "There. Happy?"
I ignored him and placed the blanket and pillow on the bed beside Javier. "It gets cold up here at night," I said, not meeting his eyes. "Try to get some sleep."
As Brad and I turned to leave, Javier spoke again, his voice small but steady. "My father will pay whatever you ask. You don't have to keep me tied up."
Brad laughed. "Nice try, kid. But we're not idiots."
We left the room, Brad locking the door behind us. In the hallway, he turned to me with a frown. "What was that about? 'Not too tight'? Since when do you care about his comfort?"
I shrugged, uncomfortable under his scrutiny. "We need him healthy for the exchange. That's all."
Brad didn't look convinced, but he let it go, returning to the main room where Tyler and Conner were setting up the burner phones for tomorrow's ransom call.
I lingered in the hallway, listening. From behind the locked door came the sound of muffled sobs Javier finally allowing himself to break down now that he was alone. The sound cut through me like a knife, raw and undeniable evidence of the pain we were causing.
I wanted to go back in there, to tell him it would be okay, that we wouldn't hurt him, that this would all be over soon. But those would be empty promises from one of his captors, meaningless comfort from someone who had helped cause his suffering.
So instead, I walked away, joining the others in the main room, trying to ignore the weight of guilt that pressed down on me with each step.
Tyler looked up as I entered. "We'll take shifts watching him. Brad and Conner first, then you and me. Two-hour rotations."
I nodded, not trusting myself to speak.
"Tomorrow morning, we make the call," Tyler continued. "By this time tomorrow, if all goes well, Mendoza will be arranging the payment."
"And if all doesn't go well?" Conner asked, voicing the question none of us wanted to consider.
Tyler's expression hardened. "It will. Mendoza loves his son. He'll pay."
The certainty in his voice should have been reassuring, but instead, it sent a chill down my spine. Because beneath that certainty was an unspoken threat what would happen if Mendoza didn't pay? What would we do with Javier then?
I didn't want to know the answer. Didn't want to consider the possibilities. So I pushed the thought away, focusing instead on the immediate future the shifts, the ransom call, the waiting.
One step at a time. That's how you walk into darkness without realizing how far you've gone. One small compromise, one rationalization, one moment of weakness at a time.
And by the time you realize how lost you are, it's too late to find your way back.
I sat on the sagging couch, exhaustion suddenly hitting me like a physical blow. The adrenaline that had carried me through the kidnapping was fading, leaving behind a bone-deep weariness that seemed to seep into every cell of my body.
But I knew sleep would be impossible. Not with Javier's sobs still echoing in my ears. Not with the memory of his terrified eyes boring into mine. Not with the knowledge of what we had done and what we might yet do weighing on my conscience.
So I sat in that dismal cabin, surrounded by my co-conspirators, listening to the wind howling through the trees outside, and wondered how I had strayed so far from the person I'd once thought I was.
The answer, of course, was simple: one step at a time. One choice at a time. One moral compromise at a time.
Until suddenly, you find yourself in a place so dark you can no longer see the path that led you there or the way back out.
Chapter 4: The Hideout
The cabin stood like a rotting tooth in the mouth of the forest. Two stories of weathered wood and broken dreams, abandoned years ago by whoever had built it with hope in their hands. The porch steps sagged in the middle, each one groaning a warning as we climbed them. Nature had been working to reclaim the place moss crept up the north-facing wall, and a family of raccoons had clearly been using the east corner as an entrance, evidenced by the small, muddy paw prints leading to a hole in the siding.
Tyler unlocked the padlock on the front door a new addition that looked obscenely modern against the ancient wood and pushed it open. The hinges protested with a sound like a wounded animal.
"Home sweet home," he said, flicking a switch that, surprisingly, brought a single bare bulb to life in the center of the main room.
The harsh light cast more shadows than illumination, stretching our silhouettes into grotesque giants against the walls. Dust motes danced in the beam, disturbed by our entrance after what might have been months of stillness. The air inside was stale and heavy with the smell of mildew, old wood, and something else something animal and slightly rotten that I didn't want to identify.
The furniture was like something from another era a sagging couch with stuffing leaking from one arm, two mismatched armchairs with the upholstery worn through in places, a coffee table with one leg slightly shorter than the others, causing it to wobble at the slightest touch. Most of it was covered with yellowed sheets that might once have been white, giving the room the appearance of being populated by lumpy ghosts.
"Get those sheets off," Tyler instructed, gesturing to the covered furniture. "And Brad, fire up the generator so we can get some heat in here."
Brad nodded and disappeared through a door that presumably led to wherever the generator was housed. Conner and I began pulling the sheets off the furniture, releasing clouds of dust that made us cough and our eyes water.
Beneath the sheets, the furniture was even more depressing faded floral patterns, cigarette burns, mysterious stains. The couch smelled faintly of cat urine despite no evidence of a cat having been in the cabin for years.
"Charming," Conner muttered, wrinkling his nose.
I glanced toward the doorway where Tyler stood with Javier, who was taking in his surroundings with wide, terrified eyes. The teenager was still bound at the wrists, his school uniform rumpled and dirty from the journey. He looked small and vulnerable in the harsh light, a child playing dress-up in his blazer and tie.
"This way," Tyler said, grabbing Javier's arm and steering him toward a hallway that led to the back of the cabin.
I followed, carrying one of the duffel bags that contained rope, duct tape, and other supplies for securing our captive. The hallway was narrow and dark, the floorboards creaking beneath our weight. Family photos still hung on the walls, though they were so faded and water-damaged that the faces were barely recognizable ghosts of people who had once called this place home.
The back room had probably been a child's bedroom once. Faded dinosaur wallpaper peeled from the walls, and a small desk sat in one corner, its surface covered in years of dust. A straight-backed wooden chair was the only other furniture, placed in the center of the room like an afterthought or like it had been positioned specifically for our arrival.
Tyler pushed Javier into the chair with more force than necessary. The boy stumbled, nearly falling before righting himself and sitting down, his bound hands awkwardly in his lap.
"Rope," Tyler said, holding out his hand without looking at me.
I hesitated, then reached into the duffel and pulled out a coil of nylon rope. It felt heavy in my hands, weighted with purpose.
Tyler took it and began efficiently binding Javier to the chair, starting with his ankles. Each loop of rope around the boy's legs elicited a small, frightened sound not quite a whimper, but something close. A sound that said he was trying to be brave but failing.
I winced with each noise, each evidence of Javier's fear cutting through me like a physical pain. This wasn't supposed to be happening. Not like this. In my mind, our "hostage" had always been an abstract concept, not a breathing, frightened human being with eyes that followed my every move.
"Please," Javier said, his voice barely above a whisper. "I won't try to escape. You don't have to tie me so tight."
Tyler ignored him, continuing to secure the rope with practiced knots. Where had he learned to tie someone up like this? It wasn't the kind of skill you picked up casually.
"The window's boarded up, and there's only one door," I said, not sure if I was speaking to Tyler or Javier. "He can't go anywhere."
Tyler shot me a look that could have frozen water. "Better safe than sorry," he said, his tone making it clear the subject wasn't up for debate.
When he finished with Javier's ankles, he moved to secure his torso to the chair back, the rope crossing over the boy's chest in a way that looked painfully restrictive.
"That's too tight," I said, unable to stop myself. "He won't be able to breathe properly."
Tyler straightened up, turning to face me fully. "You want to do this?" he asked, his voice dangerously quiet.
I swallowed hard but held my ground. "I'm just saying we don't need to cut off his circulation. We need him healthy for the exchange."
Something flickered in Tyler's eyes anger, certainly, but also something else. Suspicion, maybe. Or disappointment. Whatever it was, it made me take an involuntary step back.
"Fine," he said after a moment, loosening the ropes slightly. "Happy?"
I nodded, not trusting myself to speak. Javier's eyes found mine over Tyler's shoulder, a silent thank you in their depths that made my stomach twist with guilt.
Tyler finished securing Javier to the chair, then stepped back to assess his work. The boy was thoroughly bound ankles to the chair legs, torso to the chair back, wrists together in his lap. He could move his head and hands slightly, but that was about it.
"That should hold him," Tyler said, satisfaction evident in his voice. "Let's get the rest of the supplies inside."
We left Javier alone in the room, closing the door behind us. In the hallway, Tyler grabbed my arm, his fingers digging in hard enough to bruise.
"Don't ever question me in front of the hostage again," he hissed, his face inches from mine. "It undermines my authority."
I pulled my arm free, anger flaring. "Your authority? This isn't a military operation, Tyler. It's a kidnapping. And he's a scared kid, not a hostile combatant."
"That 'scared kid' is our ticket to five million dollars," Tyler said, his voice low but intense. "And the only way this works is if he's afraid enough to cooperate but not so terrified that he does something stupid. It's a balance, Kevin. One that I'm trying to maintain while you're in there playing good cop."
"I wasn't playing anything," I protested. "I just don't think we need to torture him."
Tyler's expression hardened. "No one is torturing anyone. But he needs to understand who's in charge. And so do you."
With that, he turned and stalked back to the main room, leaving me alone in the dark hallway with my conflicted thoughts.
When I rejoined the others, an argument was already in progress. Brad and Conner stood on opposite sides of the rickety coffee table, tension radiating between them.
"All I'm saying is that we were sloppy," Conner was saying, his voice tight with controlled anger. "The kid saw our faces. He could identify all of us."
"What was I supposed to do?" Brad shot back. "The chloroform wasn't working, and he was about to scream. We had to improvise."
"Improvising is how people end up in prison," Conner said. "We should have been more careful."
"Well, it's too late now," Brad said, throwing his hands up in frustration. "What's done is done."
"Exactly," Tyler interjected, his voice cutting through the argument like a knife. "What's done is done. The kid saw our faces. That's a complication, but not a deal-breaker. We stick to the plan get the money, let him go, disappear. By the time he can talk to the police, we'll be long gone."
Conner didn't look convinced. "And if they track us down anyway? Facial recognition is a thing, you know. And we don't exactly have the resources to disappear completely."
"We'll cross that bridge when we come to it," Tyler said with a dismissive wave. "For now, we focus on the next step: the ransom call."
He looked at each of us in turn, his gaze lingering on me as if daring me to challenge him again. When no one spoke, he nodded, satisfied with our silent acquiescence.
"Brad, Conner, you'll handle the call tomorrow morning. Use the voice disguiser, stick to the script. Five million, cryptocurrency, no police. Kevin, you'll make a supply run food, water, anything else we need. I'll stay here and watch the kid."
"Shouldn't two people stay with him?" I asked, not liking the idea of Javier alone with Tyler, especially given my brother's current mood.
Tyler's eyes narrowed. "One person is enough. He's not going anywhere."
I wanted to argue but knew it would only make things worse. So I nodded, accepting the assignment while silently resolving to be as quick as possible with the supply run.
"What about tonight?" Brad asked. "We taking shifts?"
Tyler considered this. "Two-hour rotations. Brad, you take first watch. Then Conner, then me, then Kevin. Four hours each should get us through the night."
No one objected to this arrangement. We were all exhausted, the adrenaline of the kidnapping having long since worn off, leaving behind a bone-deep weariness that made even the disgusting couch look inviting.
"I'll take some food and water to the kid," I said, reaching for one of the duffel bags that contained our supplies.
Tyler gave me a long look but didn't object. "Don't untie him," he warned. "No matter what he says."
I nodded and retreated to the kitchenette, where I assembled a makeshift meal from our supplies a sandwich, an apple, a bottle of water. Not much, but better than nothing.
When I returned to Javier's room, I found him exactly as we'd left him, bound to the chair, his head hanging forward in what might have been exhaustion or despair. He looked up as I entered, his eyes wary but not as terrified as before. Perhaps he was moving past the initial shock into something more like resignation.
"I brought you some food," I said, holding up the plate and water bottle.
He eyed the offerings suspiciously. "Is it drugged?"
The question caught me off guard. "What? No. Why would we drug your food?"
"To keep me compliant," he said with a shrug that was probably meant to look casual but came across as painfully young. "Isn't that what kidnappers do?"
I set the plate and bottle on the small desk. "We're not... I mean, we don't want to hurt you. This is just about the money."
Javier's eyes met mine, surprisingly steady. "If you don't want to hurt me, then why am I tied to a chair in an abandoned cabin in the middle of nowhere?"
I had no good answer for that. Because the truth was, we were hurting him. Maybe not physically, but the psychological trauma of being abducted, bound, and held captive that was harm, plain and simple.
"You need to eat," I said instead of addressing his question. "Keep your strength up."
I approached him cautiously, unsure how to proceed. His hands were bound together in his lap, but he could probably manage to feed himself if I placed the food within reach.
"I can hold the sandwich for you," I offered awkwardly.
Javier looked at me for a long moment, then nodded. I picked up the sandwich and held it to his mouth. He took a small, tentative bite, chewing slowly as if testing whether it was indeed free of drugs. When nothing immediate happened, he took another, larger bite.
We continued this strange, intimate ritual in silence me holding the sandwich, him taking bites, neither of us acknowledging the bizarre power dynamic at play. When he finished the sandwich, I held the apple for him in the same way, then the water bottle.
"Thank you," he said when he'd finished, the simple courtesy once again catching me off guard.
"You're welcome," I replied automatically, then winced at how absurd it sounded. Thanking your kidnapper for feeding you. Being polite in return. The social norms we cling to even in the most abnormal situations.
I gathered the empty water bottle and plate, preparing to leave, when Javier spoke again.
"The ropes are really tight," he said quietly. "My hands are going numb."
I hesitated, Tyler's warning echoing in my mind. Don't untie him. No matter what he says.
But looking at Javier's wrists, I could see he wasn't lying. The ropes had left angry red marks on his skin, and his fingers did look slightly discolored.
"I can loosen them a little," I said, setting down the plate and approaching him again. "But I can't untie you completely. I'm sorry."
I worked at the knots, careful not to undo them entirely but creating enough slack that blood could flow more freely to his hands. As I did so, I was acutely aware of how close we were, how vulnerable he was, how easily this situation could have been so much worse if Tyler had been working with different people people who saw Javier as less than human.
"Why are you doing this?" Javier asked suddenly, his voice so low I almost didn't hear it.
I looked up, my hands still on the ropes. "Doing what?"
"This," he said, nodding toward his loosened bonds. "Being... kind. The others aren't."
I didn't know how to answer that. Because I wasn't being kind, not really. I was participating in his kidnapping, in his terror. The fact that I was slightly less cruel than the others didn't make me kind.
"I'm not kind," I said finally, stepping back from the chair. "If I were kind, you wouldn't be here at all."
Javier studied me with an intensity that made me uncomfortable, like he was trying to solve a puzzle. "You don't want to be doing this," he said. It wasn't a question.
I picked up the plate and water bottle again, needing something to do with my hands. "It doesn't matter what I want. This is happening."
"You could let me go," he suggested, his voice carefully neutral, as if he were proposing something as simple as opening a window. "Right now. The others don't have to know it was you."
For one wild moment, I considered it. Considered untying him, leading him out the back door, pointing him toward the road. But the fantasy dissolved as quickly as it had formed. Tyler would know. And even if Javier escaped, where would he go? We were miles from anywhere, in dense forest, in the dark. He'd be more likely to die of exposure than find help.
"I can't," I said, and the words tasted like failure in my mouth. "I'm sorry."
Something in Javier's expression closed off, the brief hope dying in his eyes. "Yeah," he said flatly. "I figured."
I left then, unable to bear the weight of his gaze any longer. In the hallway, I leaned against the wall, the plate and bottle clutched to my chest like a shield, my heart hammering with emotions I couldn't name.
What was I doing? Who had I become? How had I gone from a community college student with dreams of a better life to a kidnapper in an abandoned cabin, feeding a bound teenager and apologizing for not being brave enough to help him escape?
One step at a time, that's how. One compromise, one rationalization, one moment of weakness at a time.
I returned to the main room to find Brad preparing for his watch shift, Conner already stretched out on one of the armchairs with his eyes closed, and Tyler organizing the burner phones on the coffee table.
"Kid eat?" Tyler asked without looking up.
"Yeah," I said, setting the empty plate in the kitchenette sink. "He ate everything."
Tyler nodded, satisfied. "Good. We need him healthy."
The clinical way he said it like Javier was livestock being fattened for market made my skin crawl. But I said nothing, too tired to start another argument.
"I'm going to try to get some sleep," I said instead, eyeing the disgusting couch with resignation.
Tyler glanced up. "Take the other bedroom. I'll wake you for your shift."
I nodded gratefully and retreated down the hallway, past Javier's room where Brad now stood guard, to the second bedroom at the end of the hall.
This room was slightly larger than Javier's, with a double bed that sagged in the middle and smelled of mothballs. The wallpaper here was a faded floral pattern, peeling at the seams and stained with water damage in one corner. A dresser with a cracked mirror stood against one wall, and a rocking chair occupied the corner by the boarded-up window.
I sat on the edge of the bed, not bothering to remove my shoes or jacket. Despite my exhaustion, I knew sleep would be elusive. Every time I closed my eyes, I saw Javier's face the fear, the hope, the resignation. I heard his voice asking why I was being kind, suggesting I let him go, accepting that I wouldn't.
The cabin creaked and settled around me, the wind outside picking up and whistling through gaps in the aging structure. From somewhere down the hall came the sound of Javier's muffled sobs, quickly shushed by Brad's gruff voice.
I lay back on the musty bedspread, staring at the water-stained ceiling, listening to the sounds of the cabin at night the generator's distant hum, the wind's mournful song, the occasional creak of floorboards as someone moved in another room.
Despite my racing thoughts, exhaustion eventually won out, and I drifted into a fitful sleep filled with dreams of running through endless woods, pursued by something I couldn't see but knew was gaining on me.
I woke with a start some time later, disoriented in the unfamiliar darkness. For a moment, I couldn't remember where I was or why. Then it all came rushing back the kidnapping, the cabin, Javier tied to a chair in the next room.
I fumbled for my phone to check the time. 2:17 AM. I'd been asleep for hours, well past when my watch shift should have started. Why hadn't Tyler woken me?
Pushing myself up from the bed, I made my way quietly to the door and opened it, peering into the dark hallway. The cabin was silent now, no sobs, no voices, just the persistent creak of old wood and the hum of the generator.
I moved down the hallway toward Javier's room, my footsteps as quiet as I could make them on the protesting floorboards. The door was closed, but no light showed beneath it. Was someone in there with him? Had they left him alone in the dark?
I hesitated, then turned the knob slowly, easing the door open just enough to peer inside.
The room was dimly lit by moonlight filtering through cracks in the boards covering the window. It took my eyes a moment to adjust, but when they did, I could make out Javier still tied to the chair, his head slumped forward in sleep or unconsciousness. No one else was in the room.
I slipped inside, closing the door softly behind me. As my eyes adjusted further to the dim light, I could see that Javier was indeed asleep, his chest rising and falling in the shallow rhythm of exhausted slumber. His position looked painfully uncomfortable, bound as he was to the straight-backed chair.
Moving closer, I could see that the ropes I had loosened earlier had somehow become tight again perhaps from his movements as he tried to get comfortable, perhaps from someone else tightening them after I left. His wrists were chafed raw where the rope had rubbed against his skin, and his hands looked swollen from restricted circulation.
Without thinking, I knelt beside the chair and began working at the knots, loosening them enough to restore proper blood flow without undoing them completely. As I worked, Javier stirred, his eyes fluttering open.
For a moment, he stared at me uncomprehendingly, still half-asleep and confused. Then awareness returned, along with fear.
