"It's okay," I whispered, continuing to work on the ropes. "I'm just loosening these. They're too tight."
He watched me silently, his eyes reflecting the faint moonlight. There was something in his gaze beyond fear now calculation, assessment, as if he were trying to figure out exactly what kind of person I was and how that might help him.
"Why are you here?" he asked finally, his voice rough with sleep and dehydration.
I finished with the ropes and sat back on my heels. "I was supposed to be on watch. I overslept."
"No," he said, shaking his head slightly. "Why are you here, in this room, loosening my ropes? The others wouldn't do that."
It was a good question, one I didn't have a clear answer for. Why was I risking Tyler's anger to provide small comforts to our captive? What did I hope to accomplish?
"I don't know," I admitted. "It just seemed... wrong. To leave you like that."
Javier studied me for a long moment, his expression unreadable in the dim light. "You're not like them," he said finally.
I looked away, uncomfortable with his assessment. "I'm exactly like them. I'm here, aren't I? I helped kidnap you."
"But you regret it," he pressed. "I can see it in your eyes. You don't want to be doing this."
"What I want doesn't matter," I said, echoing my words from earlier. "This is happening."
"It doesn't have to keep happening," Javier said, leaning forward as much as his bonds would allow. "You could end this. Help me get out of here."
I stood up abruptly, putting distance between us. "I can't. Tyler would "
"Tyler," Javier interrupted. "The one with the temper. He's your brother, isn't he? I can see the resemblance."
I nodded reluctantly. "Yeah. He's my older brother."
"And he's the one in charge," Javier continued, piecing things together. "The one who came up with this plan."
Again, I nodded, not seeing the point in denying it.
"But you don't agree with him," Javier said, his voice gaining confidence. "You're going along with it because he's your brother, but you know it's wrong."
His assessment was so accurate it felt like a physical blow. Was I that transparent? Or was Javier just unusually perceptive?
"It's not that simple," I said, though it was exactly that simple.
"It never is," Javier agreed, surprising me with his understanding. "Family is complicated. Believe me, I know."
There was something in his tone that caught my attention a hint of personal experience that went beyond the universal truth of his statement.
"What do you mean?" I asked, curious despite myself.
Javier shrugged as much as his bonds would allow. "My father has expectations. High ones. The son of Eduardo Mendoza can't just be average. He has to be exceptional. Has to be worthy of the Mendoza name."
The bitterness in his voice was subtle but unmistakable. This wasn't just teenage rebellion against parental authority. This was something deeper, a fundamental disconnect between who Javier was and who his father wanted him to be.
"What do you want to be?" I asked, genuinely curious.
A small, sad smile crossed his face. "A doctor. Specifically, a pediatric oncologist. My little sister had leukemia when she was six. The doctors who treated her they were like miracle workers. I want to be that for someone else."
The simple sincerity of his answer made my chest ache. This kid, this hostage we had bound to a chair and were holding for ransom, had dreams and aspirations that were fundamentally good. He wanted to heal, to help, to make a positive difference in the world.
And we were threatening all of that for money.
"Your sister," I said, grasping at the one detail that might offer some hope in this situation. "She recovered?"
Javier nodded. "She's fourteen now. Healthy. Normal. You'd never know she was ever sick."
"That's good," I said inadequately. "That she's okay."
"Yeah," Javier agreed. "It is."
We fell silent then, the conversation having reached a natural conclusion that neither of us seemed eager to disrupt. In that silence, I became acutely aware of the absurdity of our situation kidnapper and victim having a middle-of-the-night heart-to-heart about family expectations and career aspirations.
"I should go," I said finally, moving toward the door. "The others will wonder where I am."
Javier nodded, resignation settling over his features like a familiar cloak. "Thank you," he said quietly. "For loosening the ropes."
Our eyes met one final time across the moonlit room his filled with a mixture of fear and something that might have been understanding, mine with regret and shame that I couldn't quite hide.
Then I slipped out the door, closing it softly behind me, leaving Javier alone with his thoughts and me alone with my guilt.
In the hallway, I leaned against the wall, my heart pounding with emotions I couldn't fully name. There was something profoundly wrong about what we were doing not just legally or morally, but on a fundamental human level. We had taken a person a young person with dreams and fears and a future and reduced him to a commodity, a means to an end.
And for what? Money? Was that really worth the cost to our souls?
I knew the answer, had known it from the beginning. But knowing something is wrong and having the courage to stop it are two very different things.
As I made my way back to the main room to take up my belated watch shift, I wondered if I would find that courage before it was too late. Before we crossed a line that couldn't be uncrossed. Before someone Javier, one of us, all of us paid a price too high for any amount of money to justify.
The cabin creaked around me, the wind outside picking up into a mournful howl that seemed to echo my thoughts. Somewhere in the darkness, Tyler slept, his dreams undisturbed by the moral quagmire he had led us into. And in a chair in a back room, bound by ropes I had loosened but not removed, Javier waited for whatever morning would bring.
I had never felt more lost, more conflicted, more trapped by my own choices.
And the night was only half over.
Chapter 5: The First Cracks
Morning came like a slap to the face harsh sunlight filtering through the cracks in the boarded windows, illuminating dust motes that danced in the air like silent witnesses to our crime. I woke with a crick in my neck from sleeping in the ancient armchair, my mouth dry and tasting of stale fear. For one blessed moment, I didn't remember where I was or why. Then reality crashed down on me with the weight of an avalanche.
We had kidnapped someone. A teenager. He was tied to a chair in the back room of this decrepit cabin. And I had helped make it happen.
The others were stirring too, each of us emerging from sleep with the same grim recognition in our eyes. The initial adrenaline that had carried us through yesterday had evaporated, leaving behind jittery nerves and short tempers.
Brad was the first to speak, his voice rough with sleep. "I need coffee. Tell me someone brought coffee."
"In the supplies," Tyler said, already up and moving with purpose, as if a good night's sleep had only strengthened his resolve. "I'll get the camp stove going."
Conner sat up from his position on the sagging couch, running a hand through his disheveled hair. "What time is it?"
I checked my phone. "Just after seven."
"We make the call at nine," Tyler said, rummaging through one of the duffel bags. "That gives us time to get our shit together and run through the script one more time."
The casual way he referred to the ransom demand "the script," like we were actors in a play rather than criminals committing a felony made my stomach turn. But I said nothing, watching as he pulled out a small camping stove and a can of coffee.
"Someone should check on the kid," Brad said, stretching his arms above his head with a groan. "Make sure he's still... you know. Okay."
"I'll do it," I volunteered quickly, perhaps too quickly based on the look Tyler shot me. "I'll take him some food too."
Tyler hesitated, then nodded. "Fine. But don't take too long. We need to go over the plan for today."
I gathered some supplies from our duffel bags a granola bar, an apple, a bottle of water and made my way down the hallway to Javier's room. The door was closed, and for a moment I stood outside it, gathering my courage. What would I say to him in the harsh light of day? How could I possibly justify what we were doing?
Taking a deep breath, I opened the door.
Javier was still tied to the chair, though he'd managed to shift it slightly so that he was facing the boarded window rather than the door. At the sound of my entrance, he turned his head, his eyes red-rimmed from crying or lack of sleep, probably both.
"Good morning," I said awkwardly, immediately regretting the banality of the greeting. As if this were a normal day, as if he were a houseguest rather than a captive.
"Is it?" he replied, his voice hoarse but with an edge of defiance that hadn't been there the night before. Sleep, even in his uncomfortable position, had restored some of his spirit.
I approached with the food and water. "I brought you breakfast. It's not much, but..."
"I need my medication," Javier interrupted, his eyes fixed on mine with an intensity that was unsettling.
I paused. "Medication? What medication?"
"I'm diabetic," he said. "Type 1. I need insulin and I need to check my blood sugar. My kit is in my backpack, which I'm guessing you guys took when you grabbed me."
My heart sank. This was a complication we hadn't anticipated. Tyler had been so focused on the logistics of the kidnapping itself that we hadn't considered our target might have medical needs.
"I... I don't know where your backpack is," I admitted. "But I'll find out. What happens if you don't get your insulin?"
Javier's expression was grim. "Nothing good. My blood sugar will rise. I'll get sick nausea, vomiting, confusion. Eventually, I could go into a diabetic coma. Die, even."
The matter-of-fact way he described his potential death sent a chill through me. This wasn't just about keeping him uncomfortable but unharmed anymore. This was potentially life or death.
"I'll talk to the others," I promised. "We'll figure something out. In the meantime, you should eat something."
I held out the granola bar, but Javier shook his head. "I can't eat that without knowing my blood sugar level. It could make things worse."
"The water, then," I said, unscrewing the cap and holding the bottle to his lips.
He drank deeply, his eyes never leaving mine. When he'd had enough, I pulled the bottle away, recapping it.
"Why are you doing this?" he asked, echoing his question from the night before.
This time, I knew he wasn't asking about the small kindnesses the loosened ropes, the offered food and water. He was asking about the kidnapping itself. Why had we chosen this path, this crime, this victim?
"It's complicated," I said, sitting on the edge of the small desk across from him.
"I've got time," Javier replied with a hint of dark humor, nodding toward his bound limbs.
Despite everything, I found myself smiling slightly at his gallows humor. Then, to my own surprise, I started talking. Really talking.
"My brother Tyler, the one who seems to be in charge he's in debt. Gambling. The kind of debt where people threaten to break your legs if you don't pay. Conner's mom has cancer, needs expensive treatments their insurance won't cover. I'm about to get kicked out of community college because I can't make tuition. And Brad... I'm not actually sure what Brad's deal is. He just wants the money."
Javier listened without interrupting, his expression thoughtful rather than judgmental. When I finished, he was quiet for a moment, processing.
"So why me?" he asked finally. "Out of all the people you could have kidnapped, why the son of Eduardo Mendoza?"
The question caught me off guard. I'd been prepared for anger, for accusations, for pleas. Not for genuine curiosity.
"Tyler did research," I explained. "Your father is worth millions. You're his only child. The security at your house is minimal. You have a predictable schedule. And..."
I trailed off, not wanting to voice the last part.
"And what?" Javier pressed.
"And there was an interview your father gave last year. He called you his 'greatest achievement.' Tyler took that to mean he'd pay anything to get you back."
Something flickered across Javier's face surprise, perhaps, or a more complex emotion I couldn't read.
"My father's greatest achievement," he repeated, a strange note in his voice. "That's... not how he usually talks about me."
"What do you mean?"
Javier shifted in his chair, wincing as the movement pulled at his bound limbs. "My father and I don't exactly see eye to eye. He built Instant Pizza from nothing, worked his way up from poverty to become a millionaire. He expects me to take over the business someday, to continue his legacy."
"But that's not what you want," I guessed, remembering our conversation from the night before.
Javier shook his head. "No. I want to be a doctor. A pediatric oncologist, specifically."
"Because of your sister," I said, recalling that detail as well.
He looked surprised that I remembered. "Yes. Sophia had leukemia when she was six. She's healthy now, but watching what she went through, seeing how the doctors helped her... it changed me. I knew that's what I wanted to do with my life."
"And your father doesn't approve?"
"He thinks it's a waste of my potential," Javier said with a shrug that was probably meant to look casual but couldn't quite hide the hurt beneath. "Says I could help more people by running Instant Pizza, providing jobs, donating to hospitals rather than working in one."
The irony of the situation wasn't lost on me. Here was this kid, tied to a chair in a cabin in the woods, sharing his dreams and family conflicts with one of his kidnappers. And here I was, listening, engaging, as if we were just two people having a conversation rather than captor and captive.
"What about you?" Javier asked suddenly. "What do you want to do? Besides kidnapping, I mean."
The question startled a laugh out of me, a sound so unexpected in this grim setting that it seemed to bounce off the walls like something alien.
"I'm studying engineering," I said when the strange moment had passed. "Or I was, before I fell behind on tuition. I like solving problems, figuring out how things work."
"That's cool," Javier said, and the genuine interest in his voice made something twist painfully in my chest. "What kind of engineering?"
"Mechanical, with a focus on sustainable energy systems. I want to design more efficient solar panels, wind turbines, that kind of thing."
"So you want to save the world," Javier observed. "And I want to save kids with cancer. Seems like we should be on the same side."
The simple truth of his statement hit me like a physical blow. We should be on the same side. In another life, another reality, we might have been friends, colleagues, allies in the effort to make the world a better place. Instead, I was part of the group that had violently removed him from his life, that was holding him for ransom, that was threatening his future and possibly his health.
Before I could respond, the door opened, and Tyler stood in the doorway, his expression darkening as he took in the scene me sitting casually on the desk, engaged in what appeared to be a friendly conversation with our hostage.
"What's going on in here?" he demanded, his voice tight with controlled anger.
I stood quickly, putting distance between myself and Javier. "He's diabetic," I said, focusing on the most pressing issue. "He needs insulin and his testing kit. He says they're in his backpack."
Tyler's eyes narrowed. "Convenient timing for a medical condition to appear."
"It's not something I made up," Javier said, an edge to his voice. "Check my medical alert bracelet if you don't believe me."
For the first time, I noticed the silver bracelet on Javier's wrist, partially hidden by the ropes. Tyler approached and roughly pushed up Javier's sleeve to examine it. The bracelet was engraved with medical information confirming his diabetes.
"Shit," Tyler muttered, releasing Javier's arm. "Where's your backpack?"
"How should I know?" Javier replied. "You're the ones who took me."
Tyler's jaw tightened at the defiance in Javier's tone. "Watch your mouth, kid. Medical condition or not, you're still in no position to get smart with me."
He turned to me. "We left the backpack in the van. The van that's currently at the bottom of Miller's Lake."
My heart sank. "So his medication is gone."
"Looks that way," Tyler said, his expression unreadable.
"I need that insulin," Javier insisted, a note of genuine fear entering his voice for the first time. "Without it, I could die."
"No one's dying," Tyler said dismissively. "We'll figure something out after the ransom call."
He grabbed my arm, pulling me toward the door. "We need to talk. Now."
In the hallway, Tyler closed Javier's door and turned to me, his expression thunderous. "What the hell was that?"
"What was what?" I asked, though I knew exactly what he meant.
"That cozy little chat I walked in on," Tyler hissed. "You were sitting there talking to him like he's your new best friend instead of a hostage."
"I was trying to keep him calm," I defended. "And I found out important information he's diabetic, Tyler. That's serious."
"I know it's serious," Tyler snapped. "It's a complication we don't need. But that's not what I'm talking about, and you know it. You're getting soft, Kevin. I can see it happening."
"I'm not getting soft," I protested. "I'm just treating him like a human being."
"He's not a human being right now," Tyler said, his voice low and intense. "He's a package. A means to an end. The minute you start seeing him as a person with feelings and problems and a life outside of this cabin, you compromise everything we're working for."
"But he is a person," I insisted. "We can't just ignore that."
Tyler ran a hand through his hair in frustration. "Of course he's a person in the abstract sense. But for the purposes of this operation, he can't be. He has to be an object, a tool, a stepping stone to our goal. That's the only way this works."
"And what about after?" I asked. "When we let him go? He's seen our faces, Tyler. He knows our names at least yours and mine. He could identify us to the police."
Something dark flickered across Tyler's face. "I'm aware of that complication."
"So what's the plan?" I pressed. "How do we deal with that?"
Tyler was quiet for a moment, his eyes fixed on some middle distance. "There are ways to handle it," he said finally. "Permanently, if necessary."
The implication hit me like a bucket of ice water. "Permanently? Tyler, you can't be serious. We agreed no one gets hurt."
"And no one will, if everything goes according to plan," Tyler said, his voice eerily calm. "But we need to be prepared for all contingencies. That's all I'm saying."
"That's not all you're saying," I argued, keeping my voice low but intense. "You're talking about " I couldn't even say it. "We're kidnappers, Tyler. Not murderers."
"Keep your voice down," Tyler hissed, glancing toward the main room where Brad and Conner were presumably waiting. "I'm not saying it will come to that. I'm saying we need to be realistic about our options."
"Murder is not an option," I said firmly. "Not ever. If that's where this is heading, I'm out."
Tyler's expression hardened. "You're not out. You're in this up to your neck, just like the rest of us. And you'll do what needs to be done when the time comes."
With that, he turned and walked back toward the main room, leaving me alone in the hallway with the horrifying implication of his words echoing in my mind.
When I rejoined the others, the atmosphere in the cabin had shifted. Brad and Conner were on opposite sides of the room, a tension between them that hadn't been there the night before.
"All I'm saying is that the split should reflect the risk," Conner was saying, his voice tight with controlled anger. "I'm the one who set up the untraceable payment system. I'm the one who's handling the technical side of this whole operation."
"And I'm the one who secured the cabin, got the van, mapped the escape routes," Brad shot back. "You think your contribution is worth more than mine?"
"I think my contribution is more specialized," Conner replied. "Anyone can find a cabin and steal a van. Not everyone can set up an anonymous cryptocurrency transaction."
"Enough," Tyler interrupted, his voice cutting through their argument like a knife. "The split is equal. Four ways. End of discussion."
Brad and Conner fell silent, but the resentment lingered in the air like a bad smell. Another crack in our already fragile alliance.
"We have more important things to worry about," Tyler continued. "The kid is diabetic. His medication was in his backpack, which is now at the bottom of Miller's Lake."
"Shit," Brad muttered. "What do we do?"
"We make the ransom call as planned," Tyler said. "Get the ball rolling on the payment. Then we figure out the insulin situation."
"We can't wait," I interjected. "Diabetic ketoacidosis can develop within 24 hours. He needs his medication now."
Three pairs of eyes turned to me, surprise evident in their expressions.
"What?" I said defensively. "I had a roommate with diabetes my freshman year. I know how serious it is."
"Fine," Tyler conceded. "Conner, you and Brad handle the ransom call. Kevin and I will go into town, see if we can get insulin from a pharmacy."
"How?" I asked. "We can't exactly walk in and ask for it without a prescription."
"We'll figure something out," Tyler said dismissively. "Maybe find a 24-hour pharmacy, break in after hours."
"Break into a pharmacy?" I repeated incredulously. "That's your solution?"
"You got a better idea?" Tyler challenged.
I didn't, which only added to my growing sense of helplessness. We were in so deep now, each problem spawning new problems, each solution creating new complications. It was like quicksand the more we struggled, the deeper we sank.
"Let's focus on the ransom call first," Conner suggested, always the pragmatist. "One problem at a time."
Tyler nodded. "Right. Let's run through it one more time."
As they began discussing the details of the call the voice disguiser, the script, the demand I found myself tuning out, my mind still reeling from Tyler's casual mention of "handling" Javier "permanently." The implication was clear, even if he hadn't said it outright. If things went wrong, if our identities were compromised, Tyler was prepared to kill Javier to protect himself.
And the worst part? I wasn't entirely sure the others would object. Brad might he seemed uncomfortable with the level of violence we'd already employed. But Conner? With his mother's life potentially hanging in the balance? I wasn't so sure.
Which left me. The youngest. The one who had followed Tyler into this mess out of a misplaced sense of loyalty and desperation. The one who was now having extended conversations with our hostage, seeing him as a person rather than a "package."
The one who might be the only thing standing between Javier Mendoza and a shallow grave in these woods.
The realization settled over me like a shroud, heavy and suffocating. We had crossed a line when we took Javier, but there was another line ahead one that, once crossed, would change us forever. Would make us not just criminals but monsters.
I couldn't let that happen. Not to Javier, not to Tyler, not to any of us.
But as I watched my brother calmly instructing Conner and Brad on the finer points of ransom negotiation, I wondered if it was already too late. If the path we were on was fixed now, immutable, leading inexorably toward a destination too terrible to contemplate.
That night, long after the others had fallen asleep, I lay awake on the musty mattress, staring at the water-stained ceiling, the weight of our situation crushing down on me like a physical presence. We had crossed a line we couldn't uncross. We had set in motion events we couldn't control. We had become people I didn't recognize people capable of violence, of cruelty, of calculating the value of a human life in dollars and cents.
And somewhere in this cabin, a seventeen-year-old boy with dreams of becoming a doctor was tied to a chair, his life in our hands, his future hanging in the balance.
Sleep, when it finally came, brought no relief. Only dreams of running through endless woods, pursued by something I couldn't see but knew was gaining on me. Only this time, I wasn't alone. Javier was there too, running beside me, both of us fleeing from the monster we had created.
The monster that wore my brother's face.
Chapter 6: The Call for Ransom
The morning of the ransom call dawned gray and oppressive, heavy clouds hanging low over the cabin like nature itself was passing judgment on what we were about to do. I'd slept fitfully, waking every hour or so from dreams I couldn't quite remember but left me drenched in cold sweat.
By the time I dragged myself to the main room, Conner was already setting up the equipment for the call a laptop with a specialized VPN, a voice disguiser that looked like something from a spy movie, and one of the burner phones we'd purchased weeks ago.
"Morning, sunshine," Brad said from his position by the window, where he was peering through a gap in the boards at the dismal day outside. "You look like shit."
"Thanks," I muttered, running a hand through my hair. "Where's Tyler?"
"Checking on our guest," Brad replied, his use of the euphemism not lost on me. Guest, not hostage. Package, not person. The language of denial we'd all adopted to some degree.
Conner didn't look up from his work, his fingers flying over the laptop keyboard with practiced efficiency. "The call is set for nine. We're routing it through three different VPNs and a voice scrambler. Should be untraceable."
"Should be?" I echoed, the uncertainty in his phrasing setting off alarm bells.
Conner finally glanced up, his expression grim. "Nothing's foolproof, Kevin. But this is as close as we can get without specialized equipment I don't have access to."
The casual admission that our "untraceable" call might, in fact, be traceable was just one more weight added to the growing burden of anxiety I carried. One more thing that could go wrong. One more way this whole house of cards could come crashing down around us.
Tyler emerged from the hallway, his face set in the determined expression I'd come to dread. It was the look he got when he'd made a decision and wouldn't be swayed, regardless of the consequences.
"The kid's blood sugar is high," he announced without preamble. "He's getting shaky, says he feels nauseous. We need to move up the timeline."
"The call is in twenty minutes," Conner said, checking his watch. "We can't move it up more than that. Mendoza needs to be at his office, away from potential police presence."
Tyler nodded, accepting this limitation. "Fine. Twenty minutes. But after the call, we need to get that insulin. Today."
"How's Javier doing otherwise?" I asked, trying to keep my voice neutral.
Tyler's eyes narrowed slightly at my use of our hostage's name. "He's alive. That's all that matters for now."
The coldness in his tone sent a chill down my spine. This was the Tyler I'd glimpsed yesterday the one capable of "handling" problems "permanently." The one I barely recognized as my brother.
"I'm going to check on him," I said, moving toward the hallway.
Tyler stepped into my path. "No, you're not. You're staying here for the call. We all need to be on the same page when it happens."
I wanted to argue but knew it would only make things worse. So I nodded, swallowing my concern for Javier and the growing fear that Tyler was watching my every move, cataloging my every expression of empathy as evidence of weakness.
The next twenty minutes passed in tense silence, each of us lost in our own thoughts as Conner made final adjustments to the equipment. Brad paced the small room, his nervous energy palpable. Tyler sat perfectly still, his face a mask of calm that didn't reach his eyes. And I stood by the window, watching raindrops begin to fall, each one a tiny mirror reflecting the gray sky above.
"It's time," Conner announced finally, his voice cutting through the silence like a gunshot.
We gathered around the small table where he had set up the equipment. The burner phone sat in the center, innocent-looking despite its nefarious purpose. The voice disguiser a small black box with a microphone attachment was connected to it.
"Remember the script," Tyler said, his eyes moving from Brad to Conner. "Keep it short. Be firm but not threatening. Five million, cryptocurrency, no police. And make it clear that any deviation from our instructions will have consequences."
Brad nodded, his Adam's apple bobbing as he swallowed nervously. He would be the one speaking, his voice transformed by the disguiser into something mechanical and inhuman.
"What if he asks for proof of life?" I asked, voicing the concern that had been nagging at me.
Tyler's expression darkened. "We'll cross that bridge if we come to it. For now, stick to the script."
Conner handed the phone to Brad, who took it with slightly trembling hands. "The number is already programmed in. Just press call."
Brad took a deep breath, then pressed the button. He held the phone to his ear, the microphone of the voice disguiser positioned in front of his mouth. We all held our breath, the tension in the room thick enough to cut with a knife.
One ring. Two. Three.
Then, a click as someone answered.
"Eduardo Mendoza speaking." The voice was crisp, professional, completely unaware of the bomb we were about to drop.
Brad's eyes widened slightly at hearing the voice of our victim's father, but he recovered quickly. "Mr. Mendoza," he said, the voice disguiser transforming his words into a robotic monotone. "We have your son, Javier."
A pause, then: "Who is this? Is this some kind of joke?"
"This is not a joke," Brad continued, following the script we had rehearsed. "Javier was taken last night outside your home. He is unharmed for now, but his continued well-being depends on your cooperation."
Another pause, longer this time. When Mendoza spoke again, his voice had changed the professional veneer stripped away, replaced by something raw and primal. "Let me speak to my son. Now."
Brad looked to Tyler, who shook his head slightly. Stick to the script.
"You'll speak to him when we have confirmation that you're prepared to meet our demands," Brad said, his own voice steady despite the tremor in his hands.
"What demands?" Mendoza asked, his tone now controlled but with an undercurrent of fury that was unmistakable.
"Five million dollars," Brad stated. "Transferred to a cryptocurrency account. Details will follow once you've agreed to the terms."
"Five million," Mendoza repeated, and I could almost see him processing this, calculating, planning. "And if I pay this, you'll release my son? Unharmed?"
"Yes," Brad confirmed. "Payment for safe return. Simple transaction."
"I need proof that Javier is alive," Mendoza said, his voice hardening. "I'm not transferring a cent until I hear his voice."
Brad looked to Tyler again, panic flickering in his eyes. This was the complication we had discussed but hoped to avoid. Tyler hesitated, then nodded reluctantly.
"Wait," Brad said into the phone. "We'll put him on."
He covered the microphone with his hand and looked at Tyler. "Now what?"
"Get the kid," Tyler said to me. "Bring him here."
I nodded and hurried down the hallway to Javier's room. When I opened the door, I found him still tied to the chair, but his condition had deteriorated since I'd last seen him. His face was pale, a sheen of sweat on his forehead, his eyes slightly unfocused.
"Javier," I said, kneeling beside him and working at the ropes binding him to the chair. "Your father is on the phone. They want you to talk to him, prove you're alive."
Javier's eyes sharpened at the mention of his father. "My dad? He's on the phone?"
"Yes," I confirmed, freeing his legs from the chair but leaving his wrists bound. "But listen, you can't tell him where you are or who we are. Just... tell him you're okay and that he should pay the ransom."
"And if I don't?" Javier asked, a hint of defiance returning despite his weakened state.
I met his eyes, trying to convey the seriousness of the situation. "Then Tyler might... I don't know what he might do. But please, just cooperate for now. Please."
Something in my expression must have convinced him, because he nodded slightly. "Okay."
I helped him to his feet, steadying him when he swayed. "Are you alright? Can you walk?"
"I'm fine," he said, though the tremor in his voice suggested otherwise. "Just the diabetes. My blood sugar is probably through the roof right now."
"We're going to get your insulin," I promised as I led him down the hallway. "After this call."
When we entered the main room, Tyler, Brad, and Conner all turned to look at us. Javier's appearance pale, sweaty, clearly unwell seemed to give even Tyler pause.
"Bring him to the phone," Tyler instructed, his voice slightly less harsh than before.
I guided Javier to the table where Brad held out the phone, the voice disguiser temporarily disconnected. Before Javier could take it, Tyler grabbed his arm, pulling him close.
"Listen carefully," Tyler said, his voice low and threatening. "You tell your father you're okay. You tell him to pay the ransom. Nothing else. If you try to give away our location or identities, you'll regret it. Understand?"
Javier nodded, his eyes wide but determined. Tyler released him and nodded to Brad, who held the phone to Javier's ear.
"Dad?" Javier said, his voice surprisingly steady given his physical state.
I couldn't hear Mendoza's response, but the relief on Javier's face was evident. "I'm okay," he continued. "They haven't hurt me. But Dad, I need my insulin. My kit was in my backpack."
Tyler tensed at this deviation from his instructions, but he didn't intervene. Perhaps even he recognized the urgency of the medical situation.
Javier listened for a moment, then said, "I don't know where I am. Somewhere in the woods, I think. A cabin." He paused, listening again. "Yes, I think you should pay them. I want to come home."
His voice cracked on the last word, the first sign of the fear and homesickness he'd been so carefully controlling. It hit me like a physical blow, the reality of what we were doing to this kid not just the kidnapping itself, but the emotional trauma, the separation from his family, the fear for his life.
Tyler must have decided Javier had said enough, because he nodded to Brad, who took the phone back and reconnected the voice disguiser.
"You've heard from your son," Brad said, his robotic voice resuming. "He's alive and relatively well, though as he mentioned, he needs his insulin. His continued health depends on your swift cooperation."
"I want to speak to whoever's in charge," Mendoza said, his voice now audible to all of us as Brad had switched the phone to speaker mode.
Brad looked to Tyler, who nodded and took the phone, holding it near the voice disguiser.
"I'm in charge," Tyler said, his natural confidence amplified by the mechanical tone of the disguiser. "And I'm the one who decides whether your son comes home alive or not."
"Listen to me very carefully," Mendoza replied, his voice cold and controlled. "I will pay your ransom. I will follow your instructions to the letter. But if my son is harmed in any way any way at all there will be nowhere on this earth you can hide from me. Do you understand?"
The threat, delivered with such calm certainty, sent a chill through the room. This wasn't the desperate pleading of a frightened parent. This was a promise from a man with the resources and determination to follow through.
"Your son's safety depends entirely on your cooperation," Tyler said, seemingly unfazed by the threat. "Five million dollars. No police. We'll contact you with transfer details within 24 hours."
"The insulin," Mendoza pressed. "My son needs it. How do we handle that?"
Tyler hesitated, clearly not having anticipated this complication. "We'll get him insulin," he said finally. "But the ransom demand stands."
"Of course it does," Mendoza replied, a hint of contempt in his voice. "But my son's health is non-negotiable. I'll have a package delivered to any location you specify. Insulin, testing supplies, everything he needs. No tracking devices, no police. You have my word."
Tyler covered the microphone and looked at us. "What do you think? Could be a trap."
"We need that insulin," I said firmly. "And we don't have time to figure out how to steal it."
Conner nodded in agreement. "We can specify a public drop location, check for surveillance before pickup."
Tyler considered this, then uncovered the microphone. "Fine. There's a gas station at the intersection of Highway 16 and County Road 7. Have the package left behind the dumpster at the back of the property at exactly noon today. If we see any police, any surveillance, any sign of a trap, the deal is off. And your son suffers the consequences."
"It will be there," Mendoza promised. "No police, no surveillance. Just the medical supplies."
"Good," Tyler said. "We'll be in touch with the transfer details."
He ended the call abruptly, the silence that followed heavy with the weight of what we'd just done. We had made contact. We had issued our demand. We had set the machinery of the ransom in motion.
There was no going back now.
Brad was the first to break the silence, a grin spreading across his face. "He's going to pay! Did you hear that? He didn't even hesitate at the amount."
"Of course he's going to pay," Tyler said, his own expression one of grim satisfaction. "That's his son. His only child. His 'greatest achievement.'"
I glanced at Javier, who was still standing beside me, his bound hands in front of him, his face a mask of conflicting emotions. Relief that his father would pay, fear of what might happen next, determination not to show weakness in front of his captors.
"We need to get him back to his room," Tyler said, nodding toward Javier. "Then figure out who's going to pick up that insulin package."
"I'll take him," I volunteered, placing a supportive hand on Javier's arm. He was trembling slightly, whether from fear or his medical condition, I couldn't tell.
Tyler's eyes narrowed at the gesture, but he nodded. "Fine. But be quick about it. We need to plan our next move."
I led Javier back down the hallway to his room, supporting more of his weight with each step. By the time we reached the chair, he was leaning heavily against me, his breathing labored.
"Sit down before you fall down," I said, helping him into the chair.
"Thanks," he murmured, his eyes closing briefly as he settled into the seat. "That was... intense."
"Your father will pay," I said, not sure if I was offering reassurance or simply stating a fact. "
Chapter 7: Guilt and Isolation
I was running through endless hospital corridors, the fluorescent lights flickering overhead like dying stars. Behind me, the steady beep-beep-beep of a heart monitor grew faster, more insistent. Ahead, my parents stood with Detective Reyes, their faces masks of disbelief and disappointment as she showed them security footage of the kidnapping.
"That's our son," my mother said, her voice hollow. "That's Kevin."
"No," I tried to say, but no sound emerged. "It's not what you think. I didn't mean for this to happen."
The corridor stretched impossibly longer. My father turned away, shoulders slumped in defeat. My mother covered her face with her hands. Detective Reyes looked directly at me, her expression hardening as she reached for handcuffs hanging at her belt.
Behind them, a window appeared a hospital room where Javier lay unconscious, tubes running from his arms, his parents collapsed in grief beside his bed. Eduardo Mendoza looked up, somehow seeing me through the glass, his eyes burning with a father's rage.
I bolted upright, gasping, my t-shirt soaked with sweat despite the cabin's morning chill. The remnants of the nightmare clung to me like cobwebs, impossible to brush away completely. Three nights in a row now. Three nights of different scenarios but the same overwhelming guilt.
Across the room, Tyler snored softly, one arm flung over his face. Brad was already up, visible through the window as he paced the perimeter of the cabin, a cigarette dangling from his lips. Conner sat at the kitchen table, disassembling and cleaning the burner phones we'd use for the ransom calls, his movements precise and methodical.
Normal morning. Business as usual. As if we hadn't torn a hole in the universe by taking someone's child.
I slipped into jeans and a hoodie, moving quietly to avoid waking Tyler. He'd been on edge yesterday, snapping at Brad for talking too loudly, at Conner for suggesting we turn on the news to check for updates on the investigation. The stress was getting to all of us, but Tyler wore it differently like a fuse burning shorter by the hour.
In the bathroom, I splashed cold water on my face and stared at my reflection. Four days of stubble. Shadows under my eyes that hadn't been there a week ago. I barely recognized myself, and not just physically. The person who had agreed to this plan seemed like a stranger now some desperate, morally compromised version of me that I wanted to disown but couldn't.
I pulled the pharmacy bag from its hiding place beneath the sink, behind the ancient plunger and cleaning supplies that looked like they hadn't been touched in years. Inside was Javier's insulin and a fresh pack of syringes I'd picked up yesterday during my "supply run" ostensibly for food and cigarettes, but really a chance to drive two towns over to a pharmacy where I'd used a fake ID and cash to get the medicine Javier needed.
Tyler hadn't wanted to bother. "Rich kid like that, probably just used to getting special treatment," he'd said when Javier first mentioned his diabetes. "He'll be fine for a few days."
But I'd seen the fear in Javier's eyes real fear, not manipulation. I knew enough about diabetes to understand that going without insulin wasn't an option.
I tucked the bag into my waistband, covered it with my hoodie, and headed to the kitchen. I made a production of preparing breakfast instant oatmeal, sliced apples, coffee for everyone except Brad, who only drank energy drinks before noon. I fixed an extra bowl, making it slightly fuller than the others, adding a glob of the peanut butter we used to increase calories.
"Taking food to the package?" Conner asked without looking up from the phone he was reassembling.
"Yeah," I said, keeping my voice casual. "Figured we should keep him healthy for the exchange. Proof of life and all that."
Conner nodded, accepting the logic. That was the thing about Conner everything was about efficiency, pragmatism. If keeping Javier healthy served the plan, he was on board. No moral calculations, just tactical ones.
"Don't take too long," he said. "We need to discuss the final details for tomorrow's exchange."
I mumbled agreement and headed for the back room where we kept Javier. The key was hanging on a nail by the door a small concession to dignity, letting him use the bathroom unrestrained twice a day, though one of us always stood guard outside. The rest of the time, his hands were zip-tied in front, giving him enough mobility to eat and adjust his position, but not enough to attempt escape through the boarded-up window.
I knocked once another small courtesy that had somehow become my signature then unlocked the door and stepped inside.
Javier sat on the edge of the mattress we'd thrown on the floor, his dark hair falling over his forehead, his eyes lifting to mine with a wariness that never fully disappeared but had softened somewhat over the past days. He looked pale this morning, a slight tremor visible in his hands.
"Brought breakfast," I said, setting the bowl down beside him. "And this." I pulled out the pharmacy bag, keeping my body angled to block the view from the door in case someone walked by.
Javier's eyes widened. "You got it," he said, his voice barely above a whisper.
"Yeah. Should be the right type. I checked your old vial."
His hands were shaking too much to open the package cleanly. I hesitated, then did it for him, preparing the syringe with mechanical efficiency a skill learned from watching our neighbor's diabetic son during the years we'd lived in the apartment complex. Javier lifted his t-shirt, administering the injection with practiced ease despite his bound hands.
"Thank you," he said when it was done, the relief in his voice making my stomach twist with shame. He shouldn't have to thank his kidnapper for basic medical care.
"Don't mention it," I said, the irony of the phrase not lost on me. He literally couldn't mention it to the others without exposing my secret assistance. "Eat while it's warm."
I turned to go, but Javier's voice stopped me.
"Kevin," he said, using my name for the first time since we'd taken him. We'd been careful about that no names during the grab, balaclavas hiding our faces but I'd slipped once, and he'd remembered. "You're not like them, are you?"
I froze, hand on the doorknob, the question hitting me like a physical blow. How could I answer that? I wasn't like Tyler, maybe, with his cold calculation and escalating ruthlessness. But I was here. I had helped plan this. I had participated. My hands weren't clean just because I felt guilty afterward.
"We're all in this together," I said finally, the evasion tasting bitter on my tongue.
"But you don't want to be," Javier pressed, setting the oatmeal aside and leaning forward. "I can tell. The way you check if I'm comfortable, bring extra food, now this " He gestured to the insulin. "The others wouldn't have bothered."
"Look, don't don't make me into something I'm not, okay?" The words came out harsher than I intended. "I'm still one of the guys who took you. That doesn't change because I have basic human decency."
"Decency matters," Javier said simply. "Especially here."
I had no response to that. Instead, I changed the subject. "How are your wrists?"
He held them up, showing the raw skin where the zip-ties had chafed. "Been better."
I reached into my pocket and pulled out the travel-sized tube of antibiotic ointment I'd also purchased at the pharmacy. "Use this when you can. It'll help."
Javier took it, our fingers brushing briefly. The simple human contact sent an unexpected jolt through me a reminder that this wasn't a transaction, a criminal dealing with merchandise. This was person to person, human to human.
"You know," Javier said, his voice low, thoughtful, "my father will pay. Whatever you're asking. He can afford it, and more importantly, he loves me. This could all be over tomorrow, and no one needs to get hurt."
The earnestness in his voice made me look away. He still believed we would simply release him after getting the money. That this would end cleanly, everyone walking away intact if not unscathed.
I wanted to believe that too. But Tyler's increasingly paranoid comments about "loose ends" and "witnesses who can identify us" had planted seeds of doubt that grew stronger each day.
"Yeah," I said, unable to meet his eyes. "That's the plan."
"Kevin?" Javier said, somehow making my name sound like a lifeline being thrown. "When this is over... it's not too late for you. Whatever you're mixed up in, whatever made you do this you can still walk away. Start over."
The simplicity of his faith in his father, in the basic decency he thought he saw in me, in the possibility of redemption was almost unbearable. I mumbled something noncommittal and left, locking the door behind me, the key suddenly heavy in my hand.
I found Tyler in the main room, waiting for me. One look at his face and I knew something was wrong.
"Took you long enough," he said, his voice deceptively casual. "What's so interesting about our guest that you needed fifteen minutes to deliver breakfast?"
"Just making sure he's holding up," I said, brushing past him to return the key to its hook. "We need him in decent shape for the exchange."
Tyler's hand shot out, gripping my arm just above the elbow, his fingers digging in with precise pressure. "Funny thing," he said, his voice dropping to that dangerous register I'd come to recognize from childhood the tone that preceded losing his temper. "I went to grab the map from the car. Passed by the window to the back room. Heard quite a conversation."
My blood went cold. The window. I'd forgotten about the small gap between the boards where sound could travel. Stupid, careless mistake.
"We were just talking," I said, trying to keep my voice steady. "Keeping him calm. Cooperative."
"Cooperative," Tyler repeated, his grip tightening. "Is that what you call it? Giving him medical supplies behind our backs? Telling him everything's going to be fine? Making promises about tomorrow?"
"I didn't promise him anything," I protested, pulling my arm free. "And yeah, I got his insulin. You want him going into diabetic shock before the exchange? How's that going to look to his father?"
Tyler's eyes narrowed. "Don't pretend this is about the plan. You're getting soft. Feeling sorry for the rich kid."
"He's a person, Tyler. A college student with a medical condition who didn't ask to be dragged into our problems."
"Our problems?" Tyler laughed, the sound sharp and humorless. "This was the solution to our problems. Five million dollars. Freedom. A fresh start. That was the deal, remember? But now you're making friends with the merchandise, sneaking him extra food, medicine what's next? Helping him escape?"
The accuracy of his prediction so close to my growing, unspoken intent must have shown on my face, because Tyler's expression hardened further.
"Jesus Christ, Kevin," he hissed, keeping his voice low enough that it wouldn't carry to Javier's room. "What is wrong with you? You think any of us gets out of this if he walks? You think he won't go straight to the police, give them our descriptions, lead them right to us? You think any of us will see anything but prison walls for the next twenty years?"
"Maybe we deserve it," I said, the words escaping before I could stop them.
Tyler recoiled as if I'd slapped him. For a moment, raw hurt flashed across his face, quickly replaced by cold fury. "Well, that's where we differ, little brother. I don't think we deserve to have our lives destroyed because the system is rigged against people like us. I don't think we deserve to rot in poverty while families like the Mendozas have more money than they could spend in ten lifetimes."
"That doesn't make this right," I insisted, but my voice lacked conviction. Hadn't I made the same arguments to myself weeks ago, when the plan was first taking shape? Hadn't I justified my participation with exactly this kind of reasoning?
"No, what's not right is you turning on your own family," Tyler said, his voice dropping to a harsh whisper. "After everything we've been through. After everything I've done for you."
The familiar weight of obligation pressed down on me the unspoken ledger of debts that Tyler had always kept between us. The times he'd taken beatings meant for me when we were kids. The jobs he'd worked to help pay for my community college classes. The apartment we'd shared when I had nowhere else to go.
"I'm not turning on you," I said, the fight draining out of me. "I just... this isn't who we are, Ty. Or at least, it's not who we were supposed to be."
Something flickered in Tyler's eyes regret, maybe, or just fatigue. "Well, it's who we are now," he said finally. "And it's too late to change course. The exchange happens tomorrow. We get the money, we leave the kid somewhere he'll be found quickly, and we disappear. That's the plan. That's always been the plan."
He didn't mention the increasingly frequent conversations I'd overheard him and Conner discussing "contingencies" if something went wrong with the exchange, the need to "eliminate variables" if we thought the police might be involved. I didn't know if his omission was meant to reassure me or if he simply didn't want to argue further.
"Yeah," I said, the lie sitting heavy on my tongue. "That's the plan."
Tyler studied me for a long moment, then nodded, apparently satisfied. "Good. Now come on. Conner's got the route mapped for tomorrow. We need to memorize it."
He turned and walked back to the main room without waiting to see if I followed. I stood there, caught in the limbo between the brother I'd always followed and the prisoner whose humanity had pierced through my carefully constructed justifications.
"Everything cool?" Brad asked, appearing from the kitchen with a fresh energy drink, his eyes darting between me and Tyler's retreating back.
"Yeah," I said automatically, then reconsidered. "Actually, no. None of this is cool, Brad. How are you so okay with all this?"
Brad shifted uncomfortably, glancing toward the main room to ensure Tyler wasn't listening. "I'm not... okay with it, exactly," he admitted, his voice low. "But what choice do we have now? We're in it. Backing out means prison."
"And if something happens to Javier? If Tyler decides he's too much of a risk to let go?"
Brad's face paled slightly, confirming my suspicion that he'd had similar concerns. "Tyler wouldn't... he talks big, but he wouldn't actually hurt the kid. Not like that."
I wasn't so sure anymore. The Tyler I'd grown up with protective, fiercely loyal, sometimes frighteningly angry but never cruel seemed increasingly distant from the man who now spoke coldly about "loose ends" and "necessary sacrifices."
"Just... keep an eye on things," I said finally. "If it starts going sideways tomorrow "
"It won't," Brad interrupted, clearly uncomfortable with the direction of the conversation. "It'll go exactly as planned. This time tomorrow, we'll be in the wind, the kid will be home safe, and this will all be a bad memory."
His determined optimism might have been convincing if I hadn't noticed his hands normally rock-steady from years of mechanical work trembling slightly around his energy drink can.
"Yeah," I said, for the second time that morning. "A bad memory."
Brad clapped me on the shoulder and headed toward the main room, leaving me alone in the narrow hallway, caught between two doors one leading to where my brother waited with plans and expectations, the other to where Javier sat with his quiet assessment that I wasn't like the others.
The worst part was, I didn't know which door represented escape and which represented imprisonment. Both paths seemed to lead somewhere I didn't want to go.
________________


The remainder of the day passed in tense planning. Conner walked us through the exchange route multiple times the remote location where Eduardo Mendoza would transfer the cryptocurrency, the verification process, the planned release point for Javier several miles away where he would be found quickly but give us enough time to disappear.
Tyler was all business, focused entirely on logistics timing, contingencies, escape routes. If our earlier confrontation still bothered him, he didn't show it. Brad contributed occasional suggestions about the vehicles we'd use and abandon. I said little, going through the motions of participation while my mind worked on a different plan entirely.
By late afternoon, a heavy silence had settled over the cabin, each of us retreating into our thoughts as the reality of tomorrow's exchange loomed. Conner obsessively checked and rechecked the digital wallet we'd set up. Brad cleaned the guns we'd brought but hoped not to use. Tyler reviewed maps, marking escape routes with a red pen that looked uncomfortably like blood against the paper.
I stepped outside, needing air that wasn't thick with tension and unspoken fears. The cabin porch sagged slightly beneath my weight, the weathered boards creaking in protest. The sun was beginning its descent, painting the sky in shades of orange and pink that seemed obscenely beautiful given the circumstances.
From somewhere in the distance came the sound of birds a flock of something, geese maybe, their calls to each other carrying clearly in the still air. I watched them appear above the treeline, flying in their perfect V formation, heading south with instinctual purpose.
The sight filled me with an envy so acute it was almost painful. To be able to simply spread wings and leave, to follow an internal compass toward safety, to travel in formation with others who shared the same destination. The freedom of it. The certainty.
I had none of that now. My moral compass was spinning wildly, my destination unclear, my formation broken. I was caged not by bars but by choices some made for me, others made by me, all of them now converging toward a moment of reckoning that would arrive with tomorrow's dawn.
The porch door opened behind me. I didn't turn, assuming it was Brad with another attempt at reassurance or Tyler with more instructions. Instead, Conner settled onto the step beside me, his angular frame folding with precise economy of movement.
We sat in silence for several minutes, watching the birds disappear into the distance. Unlike Brad's nervous energy or Tyler's volatile presence, Conner's quiet was almost restful a pause in the constant tension of the past days.
"You know," he said finally, his voice thoughtful, "in game theory, there's something called the prisoner's dilemma."
I glanced at him sideways. "Sounds appropriate."
A thin smile appeared briefly on his face. "It's about cooperation versus self-interest. Two prisoners, separated, each given the choice to betray the other or remain silent. If both stay silent, they get minimal sentences. If both betray each other, moderate sentences. But if one betrays while the other stays silent the betrayer goes free, the loyal one gets the maximum."
"What's your point?" I asked, though I suspected I already knew.
Conner shrugged. "No point. Just an observation that we're all in a kind of prisoner's dilemma now. Each of us making calculations about loyalty versus self-preservation. Each of us wondering who might break first."
"Is that what you're doing? Calculating?"
"I'm always calculating," Conner said, no emotion in his voice. "That's how I survive. But here's the thing about the prisoner's dilemma it assumes the prisoners are identical, with identical values and identical stakes."
I turned to look at him fully, struck by the unexpected insight. "And we're not."
"No," he agreed. "We're not. Tyler sees this as justified retribution against a system that's failed us. Brad sees it as a one-time mistake he can outrun with enough money and distance. I see it as a logical solution to an equation of need versus opportunity." He paused, studying me. "But you you see it as something else entirely, don't you?"
I said nothing, unwilling to articulate what I increasingly saw this as not a victimless crime or a justified redistribution of wealth, but a moral catastrophe that threatened to consume everyone involved, kidnapper and victim alike.
"Whatever choice you're contemplating," Conner said, rising smoothly to his feet, "remember that in the real world, the prisoner's dilemma isn't played just once. It's iterative. Today's decisions affect tomorrow's options."
He left me with that cryptic warning, returning to the cabin where Tyler and Brad's voices now rose in some new disagreement about tomorrow's timing.
The birds were long gone, the V formation dissolved beyond my sight into the darkening sky. I remained on the porch as twilight deepened into dusk, the boundaries between earth and sky blurring in the fading light.
Tomorrow would bring choices none of us could take back. Tomorrow would reveal which of us would betray and which would remain silent, which would flee and which would stay, which would break and which would hold firm.
I only knew one thing with absolute certainty: when the sun rose on the day of the exchange, I would no longer be the person who had entered this cabin four days ago. That version of Kevin Mercer morally compromised but still claiming some tattered remnants of justification was already gone, replaced by someone who saw with painful clarity exactly what we had done and what needed to happen next.
Whether that clarity would lead to redemption or destruction remained to be seen.


Chapter 8: The Ransom Deal
The confirmation came at 3:17 PM on the fourth day of our criminal career. Conner's laptop pinged with an incoming message, and we all froze like startled deer, staring at the screen as if it might explode.
"It's from Mendoza," Conner said, his voice tight with tension as he opened the encrypted message. "He says he has the money. Five million, converted to cryptocurrency as requested. He's ready for transfer instructions."
For one surreal moment, the cabin was completely silent. Five million dollars. The number had seemed abstract before, a fantasy figure plucked from the air. Now it was real actual money, actual wealth, waiting to flow into our possession.
Brad broke the silence with a whoop of joy, punching the air like we'd just won a sporting event rather than successfully extorted a desperate father. "Holy shit! We did it! We actually did it!"
Tyler's face split into a grin that didn't quite reach his eyes. "I told you he'd pay. Never doubted it for a second."
Even Conner allowed himself a small smile, though his was tempered with caution. "We haven't got it yet. The transfer is the riskiest part."
I said nothing, unable to share in their celebration. All I could think about was Javier in the back room, still bound to that chair, his health deteriorating despite the insulin we'd retrieved from the gas station drop. This wasn't a victory. This was just another step deeper into the darkness.
The initial euphoria faded quickly, reality reasserting itself like a cold wind through the cracks in the cabin walls. We still had to get the money. We still had to decide what to do with Javier. We still had to escape clean.
"So what's the plan for the exchange?" Brad asked, dropping onto the sagging couch, his momentary excitement giving way to practical concerns.
Tyler moved to the table where a map of the surrounding area was spread out, various locations marked with red X's. "I've been thinking about this. We need a location that gives us multiple escape routes, minimal civilian presence, and no obvious places for police to set up an ambush."
"The money's digital," Conner pointed out. "We don't need a physical exchange. Mendoza transfers the cryptocurrency to our wallet, we confirm receipt, then release the kid. Simple."
Tyler shook his head. "Nothing about this is simple. We need to be smart about the endgame. That means a physical meeting point for the kid's release, one that works to our advantage."
"Why complicate things?" Brad asked, echoing my own thoughts. "The beauty of cryptocurrency is that it's untraceable. We get the money electronically, drop the kid somewhere safe, and disappear. Clean and easy."
"Clean and easy is how people get caught," Tyler said, his voice hardening. "We need contingencies. Backup plans for our backup plans."
He began outlining an elaborate scheme involving multiple vehicles, decoy locations, and a release point deep in the national forest that bordered the eastern edge of the county. The complexity of it was staggering and unnecessary, as far as I could tell. It seemed designed more to satisfy Tyler's growing paranoia than to address any real security concerns.
"This is overkill," Conner said when Tyler finally finished. "The more moving parts, the more chances for something to go wrong."
Tyler's eyes narrowed. "You got a better idea?"
"Actually, yes," Conner replied, turning his laptop around to show a simplified plan he'd apparently been working on while Tyler talked. "Mendoza transfers the money to our wallet. We verify the funds. Then we leave Javier at this fire lookout tower in the national forest." He pointed to a location on the map. "It's remote enough that we'll see anyone approaching, but it has radio equipment so he can call for help after we're gone. By the time anyone reaches him, we'll be long gone."
It was elegant in its simplicity minimizing risk while still ensuring our escape. I found myself nodding in agreement.
"I like it," Brad said. "Clean, simple, effective."
Tyler studied the plan, his jaw working as he considered it. "And what if Mendoza involves the police despite our warnings? What if they're tracking the cryptocurrency somehow? What if they have satellite surveillance on the forest?"
"Jesus, Tyler," Brad said with an exasperated sigh. "What if aliens abduct us on the way? At some point, we have to accept a reasonable level of risk."
"There's nothing reasonable about any of this," Tyler shot back. "We're kidnappers. If we get caught, we're looking at decades in prison. Forgive me for wanting to be thorough."
"Being thorough is one thing," Conner said. "Paranoia is another. And right now, you're veering into paranoia territory."
Tyler's face darkened, but before he could respond, I interjected, hoping to defuse the tension. "Has Mendoza given any indication that he's involved the police?"
Conner shook his head. "Not that I can tell. I've been monitoring police frequencies and news sites. There's been no public announcement about Javier's kidnapping, which is unusual. In most cases, the family goes public immediately, works with law enforcement to set up tip lines, that sort of thing."
"See?" Tyler said triumphantly. "That's suspicious. If he's not going public, it's because he's working with the cops behind the scenes."
"Or," I countered, "it's because we specifically told him not to involve the police, and he's terrified for his son's life. Not everything is a conspiracy, Tyler."
My brother gave me a look that was equal parts disappointment and suspicion. "When did you become so naive, Kevin? Of course Mendoza called the cops. Any parent would. The question is how we deal with that reality."
"I've been researching police procedures for kidnapping cases," Conner said, scrolling through something on his laptop. "Standard protocol is to trace the ransom call, set up surveillance on the family, monitor their financial transactions, and prepare for the exchange. They'll be looking for the moment when we're most exposed."
"Which is why my plan makes more sense," Tyler insisted. "Multiple vehicles, decoy locations, contingency routes. We need to be unpredictable."
"Or we could just keep it simple," Brad argued. "Get the money electronically, drop the kid, disappear. The more complicated the plan, the more chances for something to go wrong."
The debate continued in circles, each of us approaching the problem from different angles. Brad and Conner favored simplicity and speed get the money, release Javier, vanish. Tyler wanted elaborate precautions and multiple contingencies. And me? I just wanted this to be over, for Javier to be safe, for all of us to walk away without crossing any more lines we couldn't uncross.
As the argument wore on, a fundamental divide became increasingly clear. It wasn't just about the logistics of the exchange. It was about what came after.
"Once we have the money, we go our separate ways," Brad was saying. "New identities, new cities, new lives. This was a one-time thing, a crime of opportunity. We take the money and disappear."
"Exactly," Conner agreed. "Five million split four ways is enough to start fresh anywhere. I can set up new identities for all of us driver's licenses, social security numbers, credit histories. Clean slates."
Tyler was shaking his head before Conner even finished speaking. "It's not that simple. The kid saw our faces. He knows our names at least mine and Kevin's. He could identify us."
"So we go far away," Brad said with a shrug. "Different countries, even. The chances of him finding us are practically zero."
"Practically zero isn't zero," Tyler said, his voice dropping to a dangerous quiet. "As long as he's out there, as long as he can identify us, we're at risk. Forever."
The implication hung in the air like poison gas, seeping into our lungs, making it hard to breathe. No one spoke for a long moment, the only sound the persistent drip of water from the leaky roof into a bucket in the corner.
"What exactly are you suggesting?" Conner asked finally, his voice carefully neutral.
Tyler met his gaze steadily. "I'm suggesting we can't leave witnesses."
The words fell like stones into still water, ripples of horror spreading outward. Brad's face drained of color. Conner's expression became unreadable, his eyes fixed on his laptop screen as if it held some escape from this conversation.
And me? I felt something break inside me the last tenuous thread connecting me to the brother I thought I knew.
"No," I said, the word coming out stronger than I expected. "Absolutely not. That was never part of the plan. We agreed no one gets hurt."
"Plans change," Tyler said, his eyes cold. "Circumstances change. We adapt or we get caught."
"This isn't 'adapting,'" I argued, my voice rising despite my effort to stay calm. "This is crossing a line that puts us in an entirely different category of criminal. Kidnapping is one thing. Murder is another."
"Keep your voice down," Tyler hissed, glancing toward the hallway that led to Javier's room. "Do you want him to hear us?"
"Maybe he should hear us," I shot back. "Maybe he should know that his life is being discussed like it's a logistical problem to be solved."
Tyler's expression hardened. "His life became a logistical problem the moment we took him. That's the reality of the situation we're in."
"A situation you created," I reminded him. "You planned this. You convinced us it would be clean and simple. One bad thing to solve all our problems, remember? Well, murder isn't 'one bad thing.' It's the worst thing. And I won't be part of it."
"Then what's your solution?" Tyler demanded. "How do we ensure our safety once he's released?"
I took a deep breath, trying to organize my thoughts. "We release him unharmed. We make sure he knows that cooperating with us keeping quiet about what he knows is in his best interest. If we're caught anyway, kidnapping without injury means less jail time than murder. And if we're not caught, we can have normal lives instead of looking over our shoulders forever, wondering if today's the day they find the body."
My argument seemed to resonate with Brad, who was nodding slowly. "He's right. Murder changes everything. I signed up for a kidnapping, not... that."
Conner remained silent, his expression thoughtful. I could almost see the calculations running behind his eyes risk assessments, probability analyses, cost-benefit ratios. Always the pragmatist, our Conner.
"And what about you?" Tyler asked him. "Where do you stand?"
Conner closed his laptop carefully before answering. "I think Kevin makes valid points. Murder significantly increases both the severity of charges if we're caught and the resources dedicated to finding us. It's high-risk."
"So is leaving a witness who can identify us," Tyler countered.
"True," Conner acknowledged. "But I think we can mitigate that risk without resorting to... extreme measures. Javier has no reason to help us, but he has every reason to fear us. That fear might be enough to keep him quiet."
Tyler looked from face to face, realizing he was outnumbered. His expression shifted from anger to something more calculated. "Fine. We'll proceed with the exchange as planned. Get the money, release the kid at the lookout tower. Clean and simple, like you all want."
The ease with which he conceded made me uneasy. This wasn't like Tyler to back down so quickly, to abandon a position he'd held so strongly. But I wasn't about to question it, not when Javier's life hung in the balance.
"So we're agreed?" Brad asked, looking around at each of us. "We take the money and let the kid go. No one gets hurt."
"Agreed," Conner said with a nod.
"Agreed," I echoed, relief washing over me.
Tyler's response was a beat too late, his smile a degree too forced. "Agreed."
But as our eyes met across the room, I saw something in his that made my blood run cold. A calculation. A reservation. A decision held in abeyance but not abandoned.
In that moment, I made a silent vow to myself: regardless of what Tyler or the others might decide, I wouldn't let Javier die. I would find a way to ensure his safety, even if it meant betraying my brother. Even if it meant risking my own freedom.
Because some lines, once crossed, transform you into someone you can never come back from. And I wasn't ready to become that person. Not for five million dollars. Not for anything.
The rest of the day was spent finalizing the exchange plan, with Conner drafting the transfer instructions to send to Mendoza and Brad mapping the route to the lookout tower. Tyler participated, offering suggestions and pointing out potential weaknesses, but there was a detachment to him now, as if he were merely going through the motions.
And me? I watched my brother like a hawk, looking for signs of what he might be planning. Because despite his verbal agreement, despite his apparent cooperation, I knew Tyler wasn't done. The look in his eyes had told me everything I needed to know.
He had no intention of letting Javier walk away from this.
Which meant I needed to be ready. Ready to protect Javier, ready to stop Tyler, ready to make choices I never thought I'd have to make.
As night fell and the others prepared for sleep, I found myself standing at the cabin's small window, staring out at the darkness beyond. Somewhere out there was the world we'd left behind a world of laws and consequences, of moral boundaries and ethical constraints. A world that seemed increasingly distant with each day we spent in this cabin, with each step deeper into the darkness we'd created.
But it was still there. Still waiting. And soon, one way or another, we would have to return to it and face what we had become.
Chapter 9: Tyler's Breaking Point
The memory came to me unbidden as I watched Tyler through the cabin window, pacing the perimeter in the gray dawn light, checking and rechecking the surroundings with the intensity of a man convinced enemies lurked behind every tree.
I was eight years old, small for my age and painfully shy. The neighborhood bullies three boys from the next street over, all older and bigger than me had cornered me in the vacant lot where we sometimes played baseball. They wanted my new sneakers, the ones Mom had saved for months to buy me.
I remember the fear, the humiliation, the certainty that I was about to be beaten and robbed. And then Tyler was there, appearing like some avenging angel, though he was only twelve himself and not much bigger than the smallest of my tormentors.
"Leave my brother alone," he'd said, his voice steady despite being outnumbered.
They'd laughed, of course. Three against one were odds they liked. But Tyler didn't back down. He stood his ground, fists clenched, eyes blazing with a protective fury I'd never seen before.
The fight was brief and brutal. Tyler got in some good hits, but numbers won out in the end. They left him bleeding on the dirt, his left eye swollen shut, his lip split, his ribs bruised. But they left without my sneakers.
When I helped him home, crying more than he was, he'd squeezed my shoulder with a gentleness that belied his injuries. "Don't worry about it, Kev. That's what big brothers do. We protect what matters."
The scar above his eye a thin white line that became more pronounced when he was angry or tired was a permanent reminder of that day. A badge of honor. A symbol of the brother who had always protected me, always stood between me and harm, always put my welfare above his own.
Where was that brother now? When had the protector become someone I needed protection from?
Tyler finished his perimeter check and reentered the cabin, his movements jerky with exhaustion and tension. He hadn't slept more than an hour or two at a stretch since the ransom call, his paranoia growing with each passing day. Dark circles shadowed his eyes, and the scar above his left eyebrow stood out starkly against his pale skin.
"All clear?" Brad asked from his position by the coffee maker, the question more ritual than genuine inquiry at this point.
Tyler nodded, his gaze darting to the windows, the door, the hallway leading to Javier's room. "For now. But we need to stay alert. They could be watching us."
"Who's 'they'?" I asked, unable to keep the edge from my voice. "The police? Mendoza's private security? The CIA? Your paranoia is getting out of hand, Tyler."
He turned to me, his eyes narrowing. "My paranoia is what's kept us alive this long. While you've been playing nursemaid to the hostage, I've been making sure we don't all end up in prison."
The accusation stung, partly because there was truth in it. I had been spending more time with Javier, ensuring he got his insulin, bringing him food and water, even smuggling him books from the cabin's small collection to help pass the time. Not out of kindness, I told myself, but practicality. We needed him healthy for the exchange.
But Tyler saw it differently. To him, every moment I spent with Javier was a betrayal, a weakness, a risk to our operation.
"Speaking of our guest," Tyler continued, his voice dropping to a dangerous quiet, "care to explain these?"
He reached into his pocket and pulled out several items, laying them on the table one by one: an empty granola bar wrapper, a receipt from a pharmacy two towns over, a small notebook with pages torn out.
My stomach dropped. The wrapper was from the extra food I'd been sneaking to Javier. The receipt was from when I'd gone to get additional insulin without telling the others. And the notebook... I'd given it to Javier so he could write down his thoughts, a small mercy to help maintain his sanity in captivity.
"Where did you get those?" I asked, playing for time.
"Found them in the trash," Tyler said. "The wrapper and receipt were hidden at the bottom, like someone didn't want them found. The notebook was under the mattress in your room. Want to tell me what's going on, little brother?"
Brad and Conner had stopped what they were doing, watching the confrontation with wary eyes. This had been building for days, the tension between Tyler and me growing with each passing hour, each small act of defiance on my part, each increase in paranoia on his.
"The kid needs to eat," I said, deciding honesty was my best option. "The portions you've been giving him aren't enough, especially with his diabetes. The insulin we got from the drop was running low, so I got more. And the notebook is just something to keep him occupied. A bored hostage is a desperate hostage."
"And the torn-out pages?" Tyler pressed. "What were those for?"
I hesitated. The truth that I'd been helping Javier draft a letter to his family in case something happened to him would only confirm Tyler's suspicions of my divided loyalties.
"I don't know," I lied. "Maybe he tore them out himself."
Tyler's expression told me he didn't believe me for a second. "You're getting soft, Kevin. Compromised. And that puts us all at risk."
