I held them with a love that transcended judgment, that acknowledged the complexity of their choices and actions, that recognized in each of them the same fundamental humanity that had characterized my own brief existence.
And in that holding, that witnessing, that final act of attention before moving toward the beckoning glow, I found a peace I hadn't known was possible not the peace of all questions answered or all wounds healed, but the deeper peace of acceptance, of understanding, of recognition that even in the most tragic circumstances, there remained the possibility of growth, of meaning, of some form of redemption that didn't erase what had happened but transformed it into something that could be carried, that could even, in time, become a source of wisdom and compassion rather than only pain.
The glow brightened further, filling more of my awareness, its warmth and welcome increasingly difficult to resist. I turned toward it fully now, ready to discover what lay beyond the in-between space I had occupied, curious about the next stage of this journey that had continued beyond the death of my physical form.
As I moved toward the light, I carried with me the story that had been mine the choices made, the consequences experienced, the harm caused, the attempts at repair, the final act of protection that had ended my life but perhaps, in some small way, begun a process of redemption. I carried it not as a burden but as a part of me, integrated into whatever form of consciousness continued beyond the dissolution of the body, a chapter in a larger narrative I was only beginning to comprehend.
And as the glow enveloped me, as I felt myself moving beyond the liminal space where I had witnessed the unfolding consequences of our shared tragedy, I experienced a sense of expansion, of understanding, of connection to something larger than the individual story I had been part of something that contained all stories, all choices, all consequences, all possibilities of harm and healing, all the complex, contradictory, magnificent mess of human existence.
The last thing I was aware of before surrendering completely to the beckoning light was a sense of gratitude not for the tragedy that had ended my life, but for the life itself, brief and flawed as it had been. For the opportunity to have existed at all, to have loved and been loved, to have made choices both wise and foolish, to have experienced the full range of human emotion and connection, to have been part of the ongoing story of humanity with all its darkness and light, its cruelty and kindness, its capacity for both terrible harm and extraordinary healing.
And then I let go, moving fully into the light, beyond the story that had been mine, toward whatever awaited in the next chapter of this journey that continued beyond the boundaries of physical existence, beyond the limitations of time and space, beyond the individual narrative that had both defined and transcended the life and death of Kevin Mercer.
Chapter 21: The Final Goodbye
Tyler Mercer lay on his narrow prison bunk, the lights of the cellblock dimmed for the night, the familiar sounds of institutional life distant conversations, the occasional shout, the regular footsteps of guards making their rounds fading into the background of his awareness. After eight years of incarceration, these sounds had become as normal to him as traffic had been in his previous life, as unremarkable as the chirping of birds or the rustle of leaves in the world beyond prison walls.
He was tired, the bone-deep exhaustion that came from years of confinement, of regulated days and restless nights, of carrying the weight of what he had done and could never undo. Yet sleep eluded him, as it often did his mind too active, too full of memories and regrets, too haunted by the ghost of the brother whose life he had ended in that cabin so many years ago.
Tyler turned onto his side, punching his thin pillow into a more comfortable shape, closing his eyes against the dim light that filtered through the cell door. He focused on his breathing, on the meditation techniques he had learned in a prison program designed to help inmates manage stress and anxiety. In, hold, out. In, hold, out. The simple rhythm gradually slowing his racing thoughts, easing the tension in his body, drawing him toward the edge of sleep.
As consciousness began to slip away, the boundaries between waking and dreaming growing porous and permeable, Tyler became aware of a presence in the cell with him not threatening but familiar, a sense of someone sitting on the edge of his bunk, watching him with patient attention.
"Kevin?" he murmured, the name emerging from some place between thought and speech, recognition coming before his eyes opened to confirm what some deeper part of him already knew.
And there I was, sitting beside him not the bloody, broken figure from his nightmares, not the accusatory ghost that sometimes haunted his waking thoughts, but simply his brother, whole and unharmed, looking as I had before the kidnapping, before the cabin, before everything went so terribly wrong.
"Hey, Ty," I said, my voice exactly as he remembered it the slight rasp, the familiar cadence, the nickname only I had ever used. "It's been a while."
Tyler sat up, his back against the cell wall, his eyes wide with disbelief and wonder. Some part of him recognized this as a dream, understood that his sleeping mind had conjured this visitation to process the grief and guilt he carried. But another part, deeper and more intuitive, sensed something else a presence that transcended the boundaries of his own consciousness, a connection that defied the separation of life and death.
"Kevin," he said again, the name both a question and a confirmation. "Are you really here?"
I smiled, the expression containing all the complexity of our shared history the love and rivalry, the protection and betrayal, the bond that had survived even the ultimate violation.
"I'm here," I said, the words carrying a truth that went beyond their literal meaning. "As much as I can be. As much as you need me to be."
Tyler's eyes filled with tears, years of suppressed emotion rising to the surface in this safe space between sleeping and waking, this moment outside the normal flow of time and consequence.
"I'm so sorry," he said, the words he had spoken countless times in the privacy of his cell, in the silence of his thoughts, in the depths of his dreams. "God, Kevin, I'm so sorry for what I did to you."
"I know," I said simply, neither absolving nor condemning, merely acknowledging the truth of his remorse. "I've always known."
"I never meant for you to die," Tyler continued, the words pouring out now, the confession he had never been able to make to the brother who was no longer there to hear it. "I was angry, scared, desperate. But I never wanted to hurt you. You have to believe that."
"I do," I assured him, my expression serious but not harsh. "I was there, remember? I know it wasn't intentional. A struggle, a fall, a terrible accident born of terrible choices."
Tyler nodded, wiping at his tears with the back of his hand, a gesture reminiscent of childhood, of skinned knees and hurt feelings and the simple comfort of a brother's presence.
"But it doesn't change what happened," he said, the hard-won wisdom of years of reflection evident in his voice. "Doesn't bring you back. Doesn't undo the harm to Javier, to our parents, to everyone touched by what we did."
"No," I agreed, "it doesn't. Actions have consequences. Choices create ripples that can't be called back. That's the nature of existence, the price of free will."
We sat in silence for a moment, brothers together in the quiet of a prison cell, separated by the ultimate boundary yet connected by something that transcended it love, perhaps, or the shared history that had shaped us both, or simply the recognition of our common humanity with all its flaws and possibilities.
"I've been watching," I said finally, breaking the silence. "All of you. Mom and Dad. Javier. Brad and Conner. You. I've seen how you've all carried the weight of what happened, how you've struggled to find meaning in the aftermath, how you've tried to build something from the ruins."
Tyler looked surprised, then thoughtful, considering the implications of this statement. "You've been... present? All this time?"
"In a way," I explained, searching for words to describe a state of being that had no easy parallel in human experience. "Not continuously, not with full awareness or control. More like... drifting between moments, between people, observing without being able to interact. Until now."
"Why now?" Tyler asked, the question containing layers of meaning why this visitation, why this moment, why this bridge across the divide that separated us.
"Because I'm leaving," I said gently. "Moving on to whatever comes next. And before I go, I needed to see you one last time. To have the conversation we never got to have."
Tyler's expression shifted, a flash of panic crossing his features at the thought of losing me again, of this unexpected connection being severed as finally and completely as our physical bond had been in that cabin years ago.
"Don't go," he said, the words emerging before he could consider their selfishness, their impossibility. "Please, Kevin. Stay."
I smiled, understanding the impulse behind the request, the desperate wish to hold onto something already lost. "I can't, Ty. That's not how it works. I've stayed longer than I should have, lingered in this in-between place watching the story continue without me. It's time to move forward, to discover what comes next."
"Are you... are you okay?" Tyler asked, the question containing all the concern of an older brother, all the guilt of one who had caused harm, all the hope of one who wished for peace for a loved one beyond reach.
"I am," I assured him, my expression conveying a serenity that transcended the circumstances of my death, the tragedy that had ended my physical existence. "Not in the way you mean, not alive again, but... at peace. Ready for whatever comes next."
Tyler nodded, accepting this answer even as tears continued to track down his face, even as his hands clenched and unclenched with the effort of containing emotions too powerful for the confines of this dream, this cell, this moment suspended between worlds.
"Before I go," I said, leaning slightly closer, my presence a comfort despite its intangibility, "I want you to know something. Something important."
Tyler looked up, his expression open, vulnerable, ready to receive whatever message I had crossed the boundary between life and death to deliver.
"I forgive you," I said simply, the words containing neither absolution nor condemnation but something more complex understanding, acceptance, recognition of our shared humanity with all its capacity for both harm and healing.
Tyler's breath caught, a sound between a gasp and a sob escaping him before he could suppress it. "How?" he asked, the single word containing a universe of doubt and longing. "How can you forgive what I did?"
"Not because what happened wasn't terrible," I clarified, wanting him to understand the nature of this forgiveness, its foundation not in denial but in acceptance. "Not because the choices you made that we both made didn't cause immense harm. But because I see the whole of you, Ty. Not just your worst moment, your worst choice, but all of you the brother who protected me from bullies, who taught me to ride a bike, who worked two jobs to help pay for my college textbooks. The man who has spent eight years trying to find some way to make amends for what can never be fully repaired."
I paused, ensuring he was truly hearing me, truly understanding what I was offering not a simple absolution that erased responsibility, but a more complex forgiveness that acknowledged the full reality of what had happened while refusing to reduce either of us to our worst actions or choices.
"We were both flawed," I continued. "Both human. Both capable of terrible mistakes and meaningful growth. I participated in the kidnapping too, remember? I went along with the plan, helped hold Javier captive, was complicit in his trauma. My final act helping him escape doesn't erase that complicity. Just as your remorse, your efforts to change, don't erase what happened in that cabin. But they matter. They're part of the story too."
Tyler was silent, absorbing these words, this perspective that offered neither cheap grace nor endless condemnation but something more nuanced a recognition of the complex reality of human existence, of the possibility of growth and meaning even in the aftermath of terrible choices and irreversible harm.
"I don't know if I can forgive myself," he said finally, his voice barely audible, the admission costing him something to speak aloud. "I've tried. God knows I've tried. But every time I think I'm making progress, every time I start to believe I might be worth something despite what I did, I remember your face in that moment the shock, the pain, the betrayal. And I'm right back in that cabin, watching you die because of me."
"I know," I said, compassion in my voice rather than judgment. "Self-forgiveness is harder than forgiveness from others. It may be the work of a lifetime, Ty. But it's work worth doing. Not just for your sake, but for Mom and Dad. For everyone who still loves you despite everything that's happened."
Tyler looked up sharply at the mention of our parents. "They'd be better off without me," he said, a darkness entering his voice that hadn't been there before. "They've lost one son. Having to carry the burden of the other the one who killed their youngest that's a cruelty they don't deserve."
"That's not true," I countered, recognizing the dangerous direction of his thoughts. "They've lost enough. Losing you too would destroy them completely."
But Tyler seemed not to hear, his gaze turning inward, his expression hardening with a resolve that sent a chill through whatever remained of my consciousness.
"I've been thinking about it for a long time," he said, his voice taking on a strange calm. "How to end it. How to free them from having to visit this place, from having to reconcile their love for me with what I did to you. How to finally face the consequences of my actions in the only way that matters."
"Tyler, no," I said urgently, trying to reach him through whatever darkness had descended, whatever decision he seemed to be moving toward with terrible certainty. "That's not the answer. That's not what any of us want for you."
"Isn't it justice, though?" he asked, his eyes meeting mine with a terrible clarity. "A life for a life. Mine for yours. The scales balanced at last."
"There are no scales," I insisted, desperate to make him understand. "No cosmic balance sheet that can be settled with more death, more loss. Just people trying to find their way forward after terrible mistakes, trying to create meaning from tragedy, trying to build something worthwhile from the ruins of what was destroyed."
Tyler smiled sadly, reaching out as if to touch my face, his hand passing through the insubstantial presence that was all that remained of his brother. "Always the idealist," he said softly. "Always believing in second chances, in redemption, in the fundamental goodness of people. Even me, after everything."
"Because it's true," I said, my own voice urgent with the need to reach him, to pull him back from the edge he seemed determined to approach. "Because I've seen it in you, in our parents, in Javier, in everyone touched by what happened. The capacity for growth, for healing, for finding purpose even in the aftermath of tragedy."
For a moment, something flickered in Tyler's eyes doubt, perhaps, or a glimmer of the hope I was trying to kindle. But then it was gone, replaced by a resolve that seemed to harden with each passing second.
"I'm tired, Kevin," he said, the words containing a weariness that went beyond physical exhaustion, beyond the constraints of prison life, to something more fundamental a soul-deep fatigue with existence itself. "Tired of carrying this weight. Tired of seeing the pain in Mom and Dad's eyes when they visit. Tired of knowing that no matter what I do, no matter how I try to change or make amends, I can never undo what I did to you, to Javier, to everyone."
"Tyler, listen to me," I pleaded, my presence intensifying with the urgency of what I needed to communicate. "This isn't the way. This isn't what I want. This isn't what Mom and Dad want. This isn't what anyone wants except the darkest part of yourself that believes punishment is the only possible response to what happened."
But Tyler seemed beyond reaching now, his decision made, his course set with a terrible finality. "I've already decided," he said, his voice calm, almost peaceful. "I've been planning it for weeks. The guard who works the night shift on Thursdays is always late making his rounds. The sheets are old, fraying, but strong enough. The ceiling vent is just high enough."
The methodical description sent a wave of horror through me, the realization that this wasn't a momentary impulse but a carefully considered plan, a decision reached after long contemplation.
"Tyler, please," I begged, my voice breaking with the emotion that transcended even the boundaries between life and death. "Don't do this. Don't add another tragedy to what's already happened. Don't leave Mom and Dad to bury another son."
For a moment one brief, hopeful moment I thought I might be reaching him. His expression softened, doubt creeping in at the edges of his resolve, the mention of our parents perhaps touching something that his own pain had numbed.
"Maybe you're right," he said, uncertainty in his voice for the first time. "Maybe there is another way. Maybe I can still..."
But then his gaze hardened again, his jaw setting with determination. "No. This ends now. Tonight. After you're gone, after I've had this chance to say goodbye, to hear your forgiveness even if I can't accept it. It's the right thing, Kevin. The only thing left for me to do."
"It's not," I insisted, desperate now, aware that my presence was already beginning to fade, that the connection between us was weakening as the dream state shifted, as Tyler moved closer to waking. "Tyler, listen to me. Your life still has value. Your life still has purpose. Your life still matters to the people who love you."
But my voice was growing fainter, my presence less substantial, the boundaries of the dream reasserting themselves as consciousness began to reclaim my brother. The last thing I saw before the connection broke completely was Tyler's face peaceful now, resolved, the decision made and accepted with a finality that terrified whatever remained of my consciousness.
"Goodbye, Kevin," he said softly, the words following me as I was pulled away, back into the liminal space I had occupied since my death. "I'll see you soon."
And then I was gone from his cell, from his dream, from the momentary connection that had allowed us to bridge the ultimate divide. I was back in the in-between place, watching but unable to interact, witnessing but unable to influence, a consciousness without agency in the world of the living.
Tyler woke with the first light of dawn filtering through the small window of his cell, tears drying on his face but a strange peace settled over him the peace of a decision made, a course set, an end determined. He went through the motions of his day with mechanical precision shower, breakfast, work assignment in the prison library, lunch, afternoon classes, dinner, evening recreation period. To anyone observing, he seemed calmer than usual, more settled, the restless energy that had characterized him throughout his incarceration notably absent.
He wrote letters to our parents, to Javier, to his attorney, to the prison chaplain who had tried to offer guidance and support over the years. Carefully composed messages of apology, of gratitude, of explanation without excuse. He placed them in envelopes, addressed them, left them neatly stacked on the small desk in his cell.
As night fell and the prison settled into its evening routine, Tyler sat on his bunk, my silver lighter in his hand the one possession from his former life that he had managed to keep through his arrest, trial, and incarceration, the small token of his brother that had somehow escaped confiscation or loss. He flicked it open and closed, a rhythmic motion that seemed to calm him, to center him, to prepare him for what he had decided must come next.
When the lights dimmed for the night and the cellblock quieted, when the guard on duty made his rounds and moved on to the next section, Tyler rose from his bunk with the deliberate movements of someone executing a long-planned action. He took the sheets from his bed, tore them into strips with practiced efficiency, braided them together to form a crude but effective rope. He secured one end to the ceiling vent, tested its strength with a firm pull, nodded once in satisfaction.
Then he stood on his bunk, fashioned a noose from the other end of his makeshift rope, and placed it around his neck. For one moment one final moment of hesitation or reflection he paused, my lighter still clutched in his hand, his gaze moving around the cell that had been his home for eight years and would now be the site of his final act.
"I'm sorry," he whispered, though whether to me, to our parents, to himself, or to some higher power that might be listening, I couldn't say. "I'm so sorry for everything."
Then he stepped off the bunk, the rope pulling taut, the lighter falling from his hand to clatter on the concrete floor of the cell. His body jerked once, twice, a reflexive struggle against what his conscious mind had chosen, what his broken spirit had deemed necessary. And then stillness, the final stillness that marked the end of Tyler Mercer's life, the conclusion of a story that had begun with a desperate plan and ended with two brothers lost instead of one.
I witnessed it all, powerless to intervene, to stop what Tyler had set in motion, to prevent this final tragedy born of guilt and grief too profound to bear. I watched as his consciousness separated from his body, as confusion gave way to understanding, as he realized what had happened and what it meant.
And then, unexpectedly, he was there with me not physically, for we had both left physical form behind, but present in the same liminal space I had occupied since my death. Brother with brother once more, beyond the boundaries of life and death, beyond the constraints of time and consequence, beyond the tragedy that had separated us and the guilt that had consumed him.
"Kevin?" he said, his voice containing wonder and confusion in equal measure. "Are you... are we..."
"Together," I confirmed, the word containing neither judgment nor condemnation but simply acknowledgment of this new reality. "In the in-between place. The space between what was and what comes next."
Tyler looked around, though there was nothing to see in the conventional sense, no physical environment to observe or navigate. "I did it," he said, the realization settling over him. "I actually did it."
"Yes," I said simply. "Against my wishes. Against what I tried to tell you. But it's done now."
"Are you angry?" he asked, the question containing all the uncertainty of a child seeking reassurance, all the vulnerability of someone who had caused harm and feared the consequences.
"No," I said truthfully. "Not angry. Sad for Mom and Dad, who now have lost both sons instead of one. Sad for you, that you couldn't find a way to carry the weight of what happened, to create meaning from tragedy, to live the rest of the life you were given. But not angry. Beyond that now, I think."
Tyler was silent, absorbing this response, this perspective that offered neither approval nor rejection but simply acceptance of what was, of what had been chosen, of what could no longer be changed.
"What happens now?" he asked finally, the question containing all the uncertainty of someone facing the unknown, the ultimate mystery that follows the end of physical existence.
"I don't know exactly," I admitted. "I've been waiting, watching, lingering in this in-between place. But there's something beyond it a light, a presence, a next step in whatever journey consciousness takes after the body is left behind. I was about to move toward it when I felt the need to see you one last time, to try to reach you in that dream."
"And now?" Tyler asked, his presence somehow drawing closer to mine in this space without physical dimension or constraint.
"Now we go together, I think," I said, becoming aware again of the warm glow at the periphery of my consciousness, the gentle beckoning that had been present for some time but had intensified now, as if responding to the presence of both brothers rather than just one. "Toward whatever comes next."
The glow brightened, expanded, filled more of our awareness, its warmth and welcome increasingly difficult to resist. We turned toward it fully now, brother with brother, moving beyond the in-between space we had occupied, beyond the story that had defined our brief lives and continued to unfold in our absence, toward whatever awaited in the next chapter of this journey that continued beyond the boundaries of physical existence.
As the light enveloped us, as we felt ourselves moving beyond the liminal space where we had witnessed the unfolding consequences of our shared tragedy, we experienced a sense of expansion, of understanding, of connection to something larger than the individual stories we had been part of something that contained all stories, all choices, all consequences, all possibilities of harm and healing, all the complex, contradictory, magnificent mess of human existence.
And in that expansion, that understanding, that connection, we found a peace that transcended the circumstances of our deaths, the choices that had led to them, the harm we had caused and the healing we had attempted. Not the peace of all questions answered or all wounds healed, but the deeper peace of acceptance, of recognition that even in the most tragic circumstances, there remained the possibility of growth, of meaning, of some form of redemption that didn't erase what had happened but transformed it into something that could be carried, that could even, in time, become a source of wisdom and compassion rather than only pain.
Together, we moved fully into the light, beyond the story that had been ours, toward whatever awaited in the next chapter of this journey that continued beyond the boundaries of physical existence, beyond the limitations of time and space, beyond the individual narratives that had both defined and transcended the lives and deaths of Kevin and Tyler Mercer.
Behind us, in the world of the living, the consequences of our choices continued to unfold our parents receiving the news of a second son lost, Javier reading Tyler's final letter with complex emotions, the prison officials conducting their investigation into how a suicide could have occurred despite protocols designed to prevent it. The ripples spread outward, touching lives beyond our knowing, creating patterns of consequence that would continue long after our physical forms had returned to dust, long after our names had faded from memory, long after the specific tragedy that had consumed us had been replaced by new stories, new choices, new consequences in the ongoing drama of human existence.
But we were beyond that now, moving toward whatever came next, brother with brother, consciousness with consciousness, bound by a love that had survived betrayal, by a connection that had transcended even death itself, by a forgiveness that acknowledged the full reality of what had happened while refusing to reduce either of us to our worst actions or choices.
And as the light enveloped us completely, as we surrendered fully to its warmth and welcome, we carried with us the story that had been ours the choices made, the consequences experienced, the harm caused, the attempts at repair, the final acts that had ended our lives but perhaps, in some way we couldn't yet comprehend, begun a process of healing that extended beyond the boundaries of individual existence, into whatever mystery awaited beyond the threshold we now crossed together.




     Poems from a Black Boy 


 by Kiminou Knox
























                     
                              Introductory Note
This is not just a book; it's an invitation. It's an opportunity to step into the quiet corners of my thoughts, where restless whispers come alive in the stillness of night. Here, you’ll find love and pain, sacrifice and longing, the weight of truth, and the ache of absence.
This is a glimpse into the inner workings of my mind a mind shaped by a world that speaks to me but rarely listens, that sees us but rarely sees us. Let this book be the beginning of something deeper: a journey where you don’t just read, but feel. Where you don’t just listen, but hear.
These pages are a tease, a taste of the Black boy’s mind a place where silence holds power and vulnerability becomes strength. This is the prologue to a story still unfolding. A story of lies, of breaking and becoming, of finding beauty in the truth and power in the quiet.
It’s the first step, the first breath, the first heartbeat of a journey that will leave you yearning for more. So, turn the page. Let these words pull you in. And when you reach the end, know this: it’s only the beginning.




































































Table of Contents
Identity and Resilience 4
Love and Vulnerability 13
Silence and the Unspoken 19
Self-Sacrifice and Healing 25
Home and Belonging 34
Pain and Redemption 37










Identity and Resilience
Introduction:
 This section explores the journey of self-discovery and the strength required to maintain one's identity in a world that often misunderstands or misrepresents. These poems speak to the weight of history, the quiet power of authenticity, and the courage to become who you truly are despite external pressures. Here, vulnerability becomes strength, and the process of unbecoming what others expect reveals the true self beneath.
 THE BLACK BOY LIE
I was born beneath bruised skies, a child of fire and shadow,
My skin, a prophecy the world had already rewritten.
Before I could speak, the lie was whispered in my ear 
You are danger before you are a dream.
I learned to walk the tightrope between threat and survival,
To smile when I wanted to scream, to fight when I longed to be held.
My hands, too rough to cradle innocence, too soft to bear the weight of expectation.
They made me a weapon, then feared the sharpness of my edges.
I carry names in my blood those spoken, those stolen,
Each syllable a prayer, each silence a grave.
They tell me to move on, to forget the weight of history,
But how do you forget the chains when they are stitched into your skin?
They said I was born to run, but never to escape.
Said my anger was a wildfire, never warmth against the cold.
Taught me to be a warrior but never a child,
A body before a soul, a shadow before a man.
I have watched my brothers become martyrs, their stories rewritten in ink that bleeds,
The world mourning them with the same hands that once trembled in their presence.
I have seen my reflection splinter in the weight of their expectations,
Told I am too much, too little, too lost, too alive.
But I am the ghost that refuses to be buried,
The hymn that rises through clenched teeth,
The prophecy they tried to silence, the fire they could not tame.
I am more than what they fear, more than what they praise.
I am the storm and the stillness after.
I am the truth they never speak.
I am the Black boy they tried to break 
And I am still here.


________________


The Weight of Knowing
I woke up with the sun
and felt its light dance inside me,
but the weight of knowing was already there.
It clung to my skin like the shadow of old paths,
from places I’ve passed but never truly escaped.
There is a heaviness in the air I breathe,
a reminder that the world doesn’t always wait
for me to catch up to its pace.
In my bones, I carry stories 
some mine, others borrowed 
whispered too loud to ignore.
I’ve been taught to let them simmer
beneath my skin until they burn like fire.
Sometimes, I wish to forget,
to be free from the burden of memory.
But even in silence, it speaks.
Is it enough to carry the past with grace?
Can I stand tall in a world that wants me to shrink?
Or must I fight against the weight,
the gravity of all that I know?
I’ve learned that freedom isn’t the absence of it 
it’s the willingness to stand under the weight,
to let it shape me into someone
stronger than what I was before.
And so, I breathe in deep,
and I carry it all forward.




Reflection
This poem speaks to the tension between knowing too much and still living fully. The weight of the past feels like a shadow, always present but invisible to others. While I sometimes yearn for release, I’ve learned that carrying this weight is not a burden it’s a testament to my strength. It’s about finding peace in the knowledge that I am shaped by everything I’ve lived and survived.


________________


The Forty Days
I have wandered this desert for forty days,
Each step leaving imprints that the wind erases,
As if my journey was never meant to be remembered.
The sun burns away my certainties,
Leaving only the raw truth of who I am.
I came here full 
Full of pride, full of answers,
Full of the noise that drowned out my own voice.
Now I am emptied,
Hollowed out by hunger and thirst,
By questions that echo in the vastness.
They told me this was punishment,
But I have found revelation in the barren places,
In the silence that speaks louder than any sermon.
The temptations come at night 
To turn stone to bread, to leap from heights,
To worship the false gods of comfort and certainty.
But I remain,
Stripped to bone and spirit,
Finding strength in my emptiness.
For it is only when we are broken open
That the light can finally enter.
I will return from this wilderness
Not as I came, but transformed 
A man who has faced his demons
And learned they were only shadows,
Cast by a light I could not yet see.
Reflection:
 This poem mirrors the biblical story of Jesus’ 40 days in the wilderness, but it also speaks to the universal human experience of transformation through struggle. The desert becomes a metaphor for the internal battles we face when stripped of distractions. It fits perfectly in Identity and Resilience because it explores the strength found in vulnerability and the courage to confront oneself.








The Quiet Strength
I don’t need to scream to be heard.
I have learned the art of standing still,
of letting silence wrap around me like armor.
There’s power in silence 
not in its emptiness, but in its fullness.
In the space between words, I become my own voice.
I am not the loudest in the room,
but I have learned that the loudest voices
are often afraid to hear their own truth.
I know this because I’ve been there 
drowning out my own voice,
afraid to let it break the air.
But now, I stand firm,
quietly claiming the space I deserve.
My strength doesn’t need to be seen;
it pulses beneath the surface,
like a river carving its path through stone.
It’s in the way I carry my head,
in the way I don’t bow to expectations,
in the way I refuse to be anything but what I am.
I am not the loudest,
but I am steady 
and that, too, is a form of strength.


Reflection 
This poem reminds me that strength doesn’t have to be loud. I used to think power meant being seen and heard, but I’ve learned that quiet confidence and persistence are just as powerful. True strength lies in knowing who you are and refusing to let the world define you.














 Unbecoming
I used to believe I could be everything to everyone 
a chameleon changing to fit their needs,
always bending, never breaking.
But what happens when you bend too long,
when the shape you take no longer fits your soul?
I learned the hard way that
unbecoming isn’t weakness;
it’s the beginning of finding the truth beneath the layers.
I had to break the mold 
not because I didn’t love those who shaped it,
but because I realized I couldn’t survive in a form
that wasn’t mine.
And so, I unraveled,
piece by piece,
like a book with pages torn but still readable,
still worthy of being understood.
There’s freedom in unbecoming 
in shedding the expectations,
the labels that never suited me,
the roles I was told I should play.
In the space left behind,
I find myself again 
more raw, more real,
and more in love with who I am
outside the shadow of who I thought I had to be.
And in that freedom, I rise.


Reflection 
This poem reflects a time when I felt trapped by others’ expectations. Unbecoming was painful, but it was also liberating. I’ve learned that shedding the layers of who we think we should be is the only way to discover who we truly are. 








The Rhythm of Rage and Grace
I walk a path paved with contradictions anger intertwined with longing,
A dance of fury and fragile beauty in every step I dare to take,
In the depths of my rage, I find echoes of a sorrow unspoken,
A beat that pounds the rhythm of my defiant, conflicted soul.
In moments of explosive wrath, I become both destroyer and creation,
Firing back at a world that labels my pain as inherent sin,
Yet beneath the searing heat of vengeance lies a tender yearning,
For love, for understanding, for the freedom to simply be me.
Every clenched fist is a story, every slammed door a secret lament,
The weight of centuries borne in a single, burning glance,
I oscillate between the roles of aggressor and wounded survivor,
A symphony of contradictions where rage and grace collide.
The world demands that I choose one path either the storm or the calm,
But within me, both chaos and calm coexist in a delicate balance,
In the fury of my outburst, I also find the seeds of tender hope,
A reminder that even in the darkest nights, a soft light can emerge.
I scrawl my defiance on the walls of every forgotten alley,
Each word a burst of raw emotion, a testament to battles fought,
My heart beats a relentless cadence of resilience and desire,
Pulsing with the rhythm of a life that refuses to be defined by hate.
At times, my voice is a thunderous roar that drowns out every doubt,
At others, it is a quiet murmur filled with the promise of healing,
I navigate the storm within, a journey of relentless contradiction,
Where every cry of rage is softened by a whisper of grace.
In the interplay of fury and tenderness, I glimpse my true self,
A boy molded by a world that demanded both surrender and fight,
I embrace the complexity of my being, the light within the dark,
Knowing that every moment of anger is tempered by unyielding love.
I watch the tears that follow the blaze of my unleashed fury,
And in their glistening trails, I see a reflection of hidden hope,
A truth that no amount of rage can erase or diminish completely,
For even in pain, there is a beauty that whispers of redemption.
As I stand at the crossroads of my emotions, raw and unrefined,
I surrender to the pulse that drives my ever-contradictory heart,
Acknowledging that my rage and grace are two sides of the same coin,
And this is the black boy lie.


The Mirror
He stood in front of it every morning,
facing a reflection that didn’t feel like his own.
He looked into the eyes staring back at him,
but saw someone else 
someone he didn’t recognize.
It wasn’t that he didn’t know the face,
but that he had never learned to trust it.
The mirror was his enemy,
its truth sharper than any insult,
its judgment colder than anything
he had ever felt.
He saw all the things he was taught to hide 
the shame, the fear, the anger,
the things he was never allowed to feel
because to feel them was to admit
that something was wrong.
But every day, he faced it.
And each day, he grew a little braver,
a little closer to seeing himself
without the distortion of what he was told to be.
And one day, he’d look in that mirror
and finally say,
“I’m enough, just as I am.”
Reflection:
The mirror is always an honest reflection of who we are, even when we refuse to see it. So many of us grow up learning to hate the things we see in ourselves the parts that were too raw, too real to show. But there’s a quiet strength in learning to face that reflection, to accept everything we’ve been told is wrong with us. I’ve seen friends struggle with self image, with the lies they were taught about their worth. It’s a painful journey to start trusting the reflection, to realize that you are enough even when the world doesn’t see you as such. But the day you finally accept it  that’s the day the mirror can no longer be your enemy.






















The Inner War
Every day is a battle between
What you feel you should do,
And what your soul begs you to do.
The fear of failure and the shame of success
Dance like shadows on your path,
Twisting and turning in the light of your ambition.
What if the war isn't out there,
But within?
What if the enemy is not the world,
But the stories you tell yourself
About who you are and what you can become?
Peace begins with the quiet voice
That reminds you of your power,
The voice that refuses to be silenced,
Even in the face of your greatest doubts.
Reflection: I realize that the battles I fight aren’t always external. The real war is in my mind. It’s the narratives I’ve believed, the ones that tell me I’m not enough or I don’t belong. I’ve begun to learn that peace doesn’t come from winning the fight outside; it starts from quieting the war inside.


________________


The Beauty of Becoming
You are not a finished piece,
Not yet.
You are a collection of moments,
Each one teaching you how to rise.
Each day, you choose again 
What to shed, what to carry.
What to honor, what to release.
The beauty of becoming
Is in the unknown,
In the messy,
In the unrefined pieces
That don't yet have a name.
You are still becoming,
And that is the miracle.
Trust in the unfolding,
In the space between your dreams
And the steps you take toward them.
You do not have to have it all figured out.
All you need is the courage to keep moving.
Reflection: I’m learning that I’m not supposed to have it all figured out right now. Becoming isn’t about a destination it’s about the journey, the unpolished parts of myself that still hold so much beauty. There’s power in the unfolding, in the courage to move forward without a clear map.
________________
Love and Vulnerability
Introduction:
 In this collection of poems, love is explored not as an idealized fantasy but as a courageous act of showing up authentically. These verses delve into the spaces between people the unspoken connections, the quiet understanding, and the bravery required to remain open despite the risk of pain. Here, love is portrayed as both a choice and a journey, requiring strength, compassion, and the willingness to be seen in all your complexity.


The Courage to Love
Love is not always easy,
Not the way we want it to be.
It is not the perfect romance,
The storybook ending.
It is the choice to stay
Even when staying hurts.
The courage to show up,
In all your mess and glory.
It is the moments when you are seen
And still, you remain.
The moments when you feel small,
But choose to love anyway.
Love is not a fairytale,
But it is the bravest thing you will ever do.
And even if love disappoints,
Even if it leaves,
You will never forget the strength
You found within it.
Reflection: I’ve come to understand that love is not about waiting for perfect conditions or easy moments. It’s the choice to love even when it’s hard, when it requires vulnerability, when it demands courage. And even if love disappoints, I can still hold the strength I gained from it.












The Space Between Us
We meet in moments,
In between the breaths,
The silence before the storm.
Love is not always the loud declaration,
But the quiet understanding
That passes between us like a glance.
In the space between the words
We share what we can never say.
In the space between our bodies
We touch the unspoken parts of our souls.
No distance is too great
When the heart knows the way home.
And yet, we build walls
Around the very thing that could set us free.
What if the most powerful thing we can do
Is simply to lean in,
Closer than ever before?
Reflection: I see now that love is not always about grand gestures. It’s the quiet moments, the glances that say more than words ever could. We complicate love with distance and fear, forgetting that connection often lives in the spaces we create for each other, just by being present.


________________


The Strength of Compassion
You don't need to be strong,
Not in the way the world defines it.
You don't need to bend yourself into shapes
That leaves you broken.
True strength is in your softness,
In your ability to feel deeply,
To empathize even when it hurts,
To stand tall in the face of your own vulnerability.
Compassion is not a weakness,
It is the muscle that keeps us human.
To care for another,
Even when your own wounds bleed,
Is the bravest thing you can do.
And when you meet another’s pain
With the courage of your own heart,
You heal not only them,
But yourself as well.
Reflection: Compassion isn’t about ignoring my own pain. It’s about sitting with it and using it to understand others. I’ve learned that true strength isn’t about how tough I can be, but about how deeply I can connect with the struggles of others, and still choose to love.


________________


The Pain of Unspoken Truths
There are truths we hide,
Not because we don’t know them,
But because they are too heavy
To carry in the open air.
We bury them deep,
Hoping they will stay quiet
For just a little while longer.
But truth is a seed,
And it will grow in silence,
Rooting itself into your bones
Until you can no longer ignore it.
The weight of unspoken truths
Can break you,
But it can also heal you.
The moment you choose to speak,
You step into the light of who you truly are.
Reflection: I realize now that the truths I bury inside only grow heavier with time. The silence I’ve chosen isn’t a safe place; it’s a prison. Speaking my truth may break me open, but it also has the power to heal. It’s in the unveiling that I find freedom.




________________


The Promise of Enough
You never needed to be perfect,
Only to be whole.
The pieces of you that are broken
Are the ones that teach you to heal,
To hold the cracks like treasures,
Worn but not shattered,
Weathered but still standing.
What if enough isn't something you earn,
But something you accept?
Acknowledge the spaces in your soul
Where love hasn't yet reached,
And give them time to fill.
Trust the process,
Even when the world tells you to rush.
In this moment, you are already enough.
And in the next, you will be more than you were.
Reflection:
In the rush of life, I’ve forgotten that I don’t need to strive for perfection. I am enough as I am. I realize now that my broken pieces hold the potential for growth. If I give myself the space to breathe, I’ll discover that healing doesn’t demand speed it demands patience with myself.
________________


The Smiles that Cut
They smile with faces I once knew,
But now they are strangers,
Their laughter hiding whispers of envy,
A quiet hunger to see me fall.
And I stand before them,
The same man who gave his body to them,
But I see how their smiles twist 
How their admiration is laced with hatred,
How their eyes glint with the thrill of my destruction.
They want me to be great,
But only as long as I am small enough
For them to control.
I am both their savior and their enemy,
Their joy and their curse.
They cheer for my strength,
But the moment I show weakness,
I become their prey.
And they will tear me apart,
Piece by piece,
Until there is nothing left but the echoes
Of my love,
Still ringing in the silence they leave behind.
________________


The Betrayal of a Kiss
They come to me,
With their words of loyalty,
With their promises of love,
But I know the truth they are all lies.
I see the glint in their eyes,
The way they pretend to care,
When all they want is to see me fall.
And in their kiss,
I taste the betrayal.
I thought they were my brothers,
My friends,
But they have already chosen their path,
And I am nothing more than a pawn in their game.
They will kiss me,
But it will not be a sign of love 
It will be the mark of their treason.
And I will feel the sting of it,
The poison of their false devotion.
And I will still love them,
Even as they drive the knife deeper,
Because love knows no bounds,
Even when the world is full of knives.








________________


The Prodigal
I left with pockets full of inheritance,
Eager to taste freedom on my tongue,
To shed the weight of my father's name,
To become someone entirely my own.
The world welcomed me with open arms,
Then closed its fist around my throat
When the money ran dry,
When my charm wore thin,
When my name meant nothing in strange lands.
I found myself kneeling in dirt,
Hands that once counted gold
Now scraping for scraps,
Mouth that once spoke with pride
Now silent with shame.
It wasn't hunger that drove me home 
It was the memory of dignity,
The echo of love that never required earning.
I rehearsed apologies with each step,
Prepared to beg for the smallest corner.
But the road home is never as long
As the road away.
And there he stood,
Arms open like they never closed,
Eyes seeing me before I could see myself.
I thought I needed to be worthy of return,
But he showed me I was never unworthy of love.
The feast was not for my homecoming,
But for the death of the lie I believed 
That I could ever fall beyond the reach of grace.
Reflection:
This poem draws from the parable of the Prodigal Son but reframes it as a deeply personal journey of self-forgiveness and rediscovery. It fits beautifully in Love and Vulnerability because it explores the courage to return to love, even after feeling unworthy. The father’s unconditional love becomes a metaphor for the love we must learn to give ourselves.


Silence and the Unspoken
Introduction:
 This section explores the power of what remains unsaid the weight of silence, the burden of secrets, and the pain of suppressed truths. These poems reveal how silence can be both a prison and a sanctuary, how the unspoken can shape us as profoundly as what is expressed. Through metaphors of locked rooms, hidden boxes, and quiet screams, these verses illuminate the struggle to find voice and the courage to break the silence that binds us.


The Quiet Scream"
He never let the cracks show 
not in his smile, not in the way he joked,
like a mask painted with borrowed light.
Every laugh was a mask,
every punchline a plea for someone
to see the scars that didn't bleed.
He learned early that tears were not for him,
that laughter was the only thing that could
distract from the bruises on his heart.
So, he turned every pain into a joke,
made every ache a punchline.
But inside, his chest was a battleground,
a place where the war never ended 
and no one ever knew.
He didn’t need to be fixed.
He needed to be seen.
But how do you tell someone
you’re dying behind the mask
when they only ever see the performance?
Reflection:
This poem is about the pain of hiding behind humor. I’ve seen friends and myself use laughter as a shield, pretending everything is fine while breaking inside. The hardest part isn’t the silence; it’s realizing that the people around you may never understand the depth of your pain.








"The Box"
She was always told to keep it together,
to keep her mouth shut and her emotions tighter,
like a box with a latch that couldn’t be opened.
But inside that box was everything 
every word unsaid, every tear that never fell,
every thought left unspoken,
growing into a monster she could never control.
She smiled on the outside,
wore that mask like a second skin,
but the box was full of things she couldn’t forget 
the way she was touched when she wasn’t ready,
the way her voice was stolen
before it had a chance to form.
No one asked her if she was okay,
no one noticed the weight she carried 
they just told her to keep it together,
as if holding it all in was a virtue.
But the box was never meant to be locked.
And one day, it broke open,
and everything that had been held inside
came rushing out 
a flood of words, of pain,
of stories she was never allowed to tell.


Reflection:
This poem speaks to the hidden traumas we carry. We’re taught to suppress our emotions, to “keep it together,” but those silences have a way of breaking free. The box can only stay closed for so long before it bursts open. Healing begins when we allow ourselves to confront what we’ve locked away.






________________


Poem: "The Echo"
He was the kind of boy who always seemed
to be running from something,
though no one could ever tell exactly what.
He’d laugh loud, then fall silent,
disappear behind walls he’d built
out of jokes and bravado.
His eyes were always searching for something 
for a way out,
for a way to prove he wasn’t afraid.
But what no one knew was that the fear
had been with him from the start.
It was in the way his father never came home,
in the way his mother never looked up,
in the way his sisters whispered in the corners,
the family secrets folding in on themselves
like paper cranes with broken wings.
He had learned to live with the echoes 
the empty rooms, the unspoken words,
the silence that stretched over him
like a second skin.
He never told anyone how loud those echoes were,
how the absence of love
was louder than anything he’d ever heard.
He filled the space with noise,
but the silence always found its way back.


Reflection:
I know someone like this someone who always seems to be running from something, but no one knows what it is. The trauma of an absent father, a distant mother, or a family too broken to heal these are the invisible scars that shape us. The hardest part isn’t the pain it’s the silence that comes with it, the space between words that nobody dares to address. The way we fill the silence with laughter, distractions, and noise, hoping that if we talk loud enough, the echoes won’t catch up with us. But the echoes are always there, waiting to remind us of what we’ve lost.
________________


Poem: "The Forgotten Room"
She never spoke about it 
the room that no one ever opened,
the one with dust on the corners
and a lock that never seemed to fit.
Her eyes always glazed over
when the subject came up,
as if there were a part of her
she’d forgotten how to reach.
But I saw it once 
the way her hands shook as she turned the key,
the way the door creaked open
like the sound of a truth long buried.
Inside, there were memories,
things she had packed away
and hidden from the world,
and from herself.
The photographs, the letters,
the things she thought she could forget.
We are taught to lock things away,
to pretend they don’t matter,
but even the quietest rooms will whisper
if you listen close enough.
Her silence wasn’t absence.
It was just a place she couldn’t bear to visit,
but one she would never leave behind.


Reflection:
This poem speaks to the parts of us that we lock away memories, traumas, things we think we can forget. There’s a room inside each of us where we store our secrets, our pain, and we close the door, thinking we’ve left it all behind. But the truth is, no matter how much we try to lock it up, those memories will always find their way back to us. I’ve seen friends struggle with things they can’t talk about, with pasts they can’t forget, and it makes me realize how much we carry with us without even knowing it. We think we’re free, but we’re never truly free from the rooms we’ve locked away.


________________


Poem: "The Story He Never Told"
He was the kind of man who always had a story,
but none of them were ever about him.
He spoke of adventures, of distant lands,
of laughter and triumphs,
but I could see it in his eyes 
the story he never told,
the one that made his hands tremble
when no one was watching.
It was in the way his shoulders slumped
when the night grew too quiet,
in the way his voice caught
when he spoke of his family.
There were things buried deep inside him,
things that even he wasn’t sure how to name,
things that made him look in the mirror
and question who he was,
who he could have been,
if only he had been allowed to grow.
I never asked him what he was running from 
maybe because I already knew.
He had been carrying it for so long
that it had become part of him.
The story was too painful to tell,
and so he kept it locked inside,
trading it for tales of laughter
and pretending that the silence didn’t scream.


Reflection:
I think about how many of us live with untold stories, trapped in the space between our breaths and our words. We cover our scars with humor, with triumphs, with anything that distracts from the things we carry. I’ve seen friends like this people who wear the weight of their pasts, but never let anyone see it. They tell stories about everyone else, but never about themselves, because their own story is too painful to share. I understand that pain. There are things inside us that we think we can never speak aloud. But those untold stories are the ones that define us, whether we say them or not.


________________


The Silent Scream
There is a voice in me that they cannot hear 
A scream that rattles my bones,
A cry that no one knows exists.
I stand here,
Among them,
Smiling,
Nodding,
Pretending that I am part of the crowd.
But inside, I am unraveling.
Inside, I am tearing myself apart for them 
For their peace,
For their joy,
For their salvation.
But they do not know how much it costs.
They cannot see the threads of my soul
Being pulled taut with each sacrifice I make.
I want to scream,
To shout,
To rage at the world that demands so much of me.
But I cannot 
For in their eyes, I am nothing more than a sacrifice,
A tool,
A means to their end.
So I remain silent,
Even as the scream inside me grows louder,
Until it drowns out everything else,
Until I am no longer sure where I end
And they begin.


________________


The Weight of the Unspoken
I walk among them,
And they cheer,
Yet their cheers are sharp,
Like broken glass beneath my feet 
They don’t see the blood,
The blood I’ve already spilled for them,
Spilled without asking,
Without hesitation.
I have offered them what they cannot take,
What they will never understand,
Not now, not ever.
They laugh and clap,
But their applause sounds empty,
Like a song with no melody,
A prayer with no voice.
I give them my every breath,
My heart,
And they worship what I am not,
Praising what they think they see.
They hold their hands out to me,
But those hands are not open 
They are fists wrapped in confusion.
Do they even know I am dying?
Or is my death their victory?
I am nothing but a face to them,
A tool for their own desire,
A means to an end they never asked me about,
But somehow, I keep giving 
Because that is what love does,
Even when no one asks for it.
________________
Self-Sacrifice and Healing
Introduction:
 These poems explore the delicate balance between giving to others and preserving the self. They speak to the paradox of love that both heals and breaks, the weight of expectations, and the journey toward wholeness. Through powerful imagery of empty vessels, thorned crowns, and healing hands, this section examines the cost of self-sacrifice and the path toward healing not just for others, but for oneself.
The Hands That Heal and Break
They come to me with hands outstretched,
But mine are scarred from holding too much,
Worn from carrying the weight of souls
Begging for what I cannot give.
What do they want from me?
Is it hope?
Is it a miracle?
Or is it simply to ease their suffering,
Even if that means destroying me in the process?
I lay my hands on them 
And they think they are healed.
But the truth is,
I am the one who bleeds for them.
I give,
But they don’t see the weight of it 
The cost of my love.
They want the freedom that comes without the chains,
The peace that comes without the struggle,
And they take it 
Even as they tear me apart in the process.
I heal them,
But in healing them,
I destroy myself 
The paradox of love,
To give what can never be returned.
And when I am gone,
Will they remember what I gave?
Or will they simply carry on,
Blind to the scars I left in my wake?
________________


The Empty Vessel
I have been emptied so many times,
Yet they come, filling me with their needs.
I give until there’s nothing left,
Just the hollow echo of who I was. 
But even then,
They do not stop.
They want more,
More than I can offer,
More than I have to give.
I am nothing but a vessel now,
A container for their wants,
For their dreams.
And I watch as they drink from me,
But never once do they see how dry I’ve become.
They take from me,
And I let them 
Because love means giving even when there is nothing left to give.
But what happens when the vessel is empty?
What happens when the one who gave it all
Has nothing left to offer?


________________


The Thorned Crown
They crown me with their love,
But their love is not a gentle thing 
It is thorns,
Pressed into my flesh,
Piercing my skull,
Until the blood flows down my face.
They worship my sacrifice,
But do they understand it?
Do they see the pain in my eyes,
The tears I’ve shed alone,
The cost of this love they take without giving?
I wear the crown of their desire 
A crown made not of gold,
But of broken dreams,
Of promises unkept,
Of hands that reach but never touch.
And they look at me with reverence,
But they do not know the weight of this crown,
How it presses against my brow,
How it cuts into my mind.
They ask for everything,
And I give,
But in giving, I become less than I was 
Until I am nothing but a shadow of the man
They never truly knew.


________________


The Empty Vessel
I have been emptied so many times,
But still they come 
Filling me with their expectations,
Their desires,
Their needs.
I give and give,
Until there is nothing left but the hollow echo
Of who I once was.
But even then,
They do not stop.
They want more,
More than I can offer,
More than I have to give.
I am nothing but a vessel now,
A container for their wants,
For their dreams.
And I watch as they drink from me,
But never once do they see how dry I’ve become.
They take from me,
And I let them 
Because love means giving even when there is nothing left to give.
But what happens when the vessel is empty?
What happens when the one who gave it all
Has nothing left to offer?




________________


The Final Breath
And when it is over 
When the crowd has gone silent,
When the weight of the world is finally lifted,
What will they remember?
Will they remember the love I gave?
Will they remember the agony I bore for them?
Or will they forget,
As they always do,
And move on,
Leaving me behind in the dust?
I have given them everything 
My body,
My heart,
My soul.
And still they do not see.
But when I am gone,
When the dust settles and the silence falls,
I will rise again,
Not as they wanted,
But as the love they never knew they needed.
And they will remember,
In the quiet moments,
In the spaces where their hearts still ache,
That I loved them,
Even when they never asked me to.


Reflection
These poems dive into the complex, often painful paradox of selfsacrifice and selflove. They explore the tension between giving everything to others whether through love, compassion, or duty and the exhaustion, the breaking of oneself in the process. There is a clear interplay between the desire to love unconditionally and the toll it takes when that love is unreciprocated or misunderstood. The speaker struggles to reconcile the divine call to love with the human cost of that love, and the painful truth that, sometimes, giving everything means losing yourself entirely. The journey through these poems is not just one of selfdestruction, but also a reflection on the struggle to remain human while carrying the weight of the world.
________________


The Art of Letting Go"
I have learned to let go of form,
to move like water 
not fixed, not rigid,
but flowing in the shape of whatever it needs to be.
To be formless is to be free,
to shapeshift into whatever the moment demands.
I am not bound by expectation,
not trapped by tradition.
I am the wind that cannot be caught,
the shadow that cannot be held.
And in this formlessness,
I find my strength.
For when you assume nothing,
you become everything.










________________


The Gift of Time
Time is both a blessing and a curse,
A river that carries us forward,
But also pulls us under.
We rush to meet its demands,
Forgetful of the moments we waste,
The minutes we cannot reclaim.
But time is also a gift 
A reminder that every day
Is an opportunity to start anew.
The past is not where you live,
Nor is the future yours to hold.
The gift of time is the ability to choose,
To make each moment count,
To love without regret,
To give without expectation,
And to live with intention.
Reflection: Time is the most precious gift I have, yet I often forget its value. The past is behind me, and the future is uncertain. The only power I have is in how I use the present. Each moment is a new chance to choose how I show up in the world, and that’s all I can control.


________________


The Weight of Your Own Word
Words are more than just sounds,
They are the weight of your soul,
A reflection of your innermost truth,
Or the silence you refuse to acknowledge.
We carry our words like bricks,
Building walls around us,
Protecting our fragile hearts from the outside world.
But what if the walls we built
Are the ones that keep us in?
What if our silence is the chains
We choose to wear every day?
Speak only what uplifts,
What breaks chains and heals hearts.
Let your words be a bridge,
Not a barrier.
Reflection: Every word I speak has the power to either free or imprison me. I carry them like a sacred force, remembering that silence or speech both have consequences. In every moment, my words reflect my truth, and I have the power to choose what I say and what I leave unsaid.


________________


The Gift of Time
Time is both a blessing and a curse,
A river that carries us forward,
But also pulls us under.
We rush to meet its demands,
Forgetful of the moments we waste,

________________
